Three days later, Kara returns home from a bath in the creek just as the sun is about to set. Alex is finishing repainting the old sign above the door. Danvers Footwear, est. 1880 shines in a new, deep blue, and underneath it next to the window there is a new, freshly painted sign: We outfit for the Klondike. Buy one pair of mining boots with two pairs of wool socks for $4,95.

Alex is grinning up at her when she sees her and wipes the sweat out of her face. “You’re back. Did you pick any blackberries or did the bears eat ‘em all?”

Kara laughs and holds up the jar of berries in triumph. “I defeated a bear in hand-to-hand combat just for you to get ‘em!”

Alex does a motion of flicking paint at Kara’s freshly washed hair, and Kara shrieks “You daren’t!”, but they both erupt in laughter.

“You should visit the creek too, Alex. The water is cold and it’s as peaceful as ever up there,” Kara says softly when they’ve collected themselves.

They’ve been working almost around the clock, making shoes during the day, running errands and doing housework in the early mornings and at night. There are no long lines at their counter anymore, to their relief, but they still get a handful of new orders each day. Especially Alex has been reluctant to enjoy even a few minutes of leisure. Kara feels that her sister is trying to prove herself as the business owner, as if trying to make up for the lost time of the previous years.

“I will,” Alex promises and wipes at her face again. She looks tired suddenly. “I will go with the first daylight of the morning.”

“Come inside and get washed up. I will put the blackberries in the ice box, and then make dinner. Has Charlie returned to the boarding house already?” Kara asks.

Alex lifts up the bucket of paint to carry it inside. “No, he is in the kitchen. He is practicing his ABCs.” She smiles, and Kara returns it.

In the short amount of time that Charlie has helped them at the shop, it has become clear how much Alex adores the young boy. He is quick-witted and a fast learner. He mostly helps Kara on the sewing machine, but they can see him doing more soon. He doesn’t talk much of his past or his family, but if anything, Kara and Alex can understand that better than anyone. Instead, he likes to make them laugh by making silly faces at them over their sandwiches on their lunch break.

When Kara walks into the kitchen, Charlie doesn’t look up. He is too engrossed in his writing, his tongue between his teeth and a tight grip on the pencil. Kara laughs to herself. “Hello, buddy! How’s your writing exercise going?”

“Kara!” The boy exclaims and holds up the sheet of paper. Big, wry letters are scattered across the page, his hand still unsteady and clumsy.

Alex had suggested she teach him the alphabet the night before, and Charlie had agreed with great enthusiasm. Kara had gone upstairs to give him the writing paper she had once received from Lena. It is precious to her, but seeing Charlie use it fills her with more joy than her own unrefined attempts at poems that she would rip out and stuff into a drawer on her side of the vanity.

She takes a look at the C H A R L I E, A L E X, K A R A and E L I Z A and smiles at him. “Good job, buddy! You’re eating dinner with us?”

Charlie nods and smiles. He waits for Kara to put the jar of blackberries away before he speaks. “Miss Jackson has been making a fuss. All the rooms are full, new people come and go every day. Everybody wants to look for gold. She has no time for me.” His face turns solemn.

“Well, we have time for you, buddy.” she says softly, and Charlie looks up to her with a smile.

She goes to wash her hands, then starts to prepare dinner. She has just cut the potatoes and onions and is about to heat up butter in the frying pan when there is a knock on the shop’s door. She suppresses a groan. They have enough work, and she wants to have the food ready before Eliza comes home.

Alex has just come in from the backdoor, standing underneath the stairs in her undergarment. She gives Kara a look. “Do not answer it, Kara. Please. I just wanna put my feet up.”

Kara hesitates. There is another knock and a voice that calls out. “I’m not a client. I’m Samantha, a friend of Lena’s.”

Kara’s eyes go wide, and she dashes toward the door. She fumbles with the lock in her haste to open it.

“Miss Samantha!” she exclaims once the door is open and she is face to face with Lena’s friend. She has only met her once briefly, at Winn’s shop over a year ago. It feels like a lifetime ago. Her eyes dart to the girl standing next to Samantha. It must be Ruby, she thinks. Lena would talk about the two of them sometimes. The girl is taller than Kara had imagined. Her skin is tan, and she eyes Kara with noticeable curiosity.

Kara quickly looks back to the girl’s mother. “What brings you here, Miss Samantha?”

The woman laughs. “Just Sam is fine. You’re Kara, right?”

Kara nods, but something tells her that Sam only asks to be polite. She can feel crimson creep up her neck. Has Lena confided in her friend about her? The smallest suspicion of Sam knowing of the bond she shares with Lena makes her want to slam the door shut, but she puts on a smile and lets the two in.

The workshop is dark, and she quickly leads them into the back. When they enter, she can hear Alex’ quick footsteps retreating onto the upstairs on the old wooden planks. She doesn’t lead them into the kitchen but instead stops where Alex had been standing earlier. The narrow hallway is illuminated only from the gas lamp in the kitchen and the light coming in from the backdoor that Alex had left ajar.

Sam’s eyes go wide when she sees Charlie sitting in the kitchen, but Kara feels too exhausted to introduce them. Ruby, however, walks into the room like she has always lived here and starts to talk animatedly to him.

“Excuse her behavior, Ruby can be-“ Sam starts to apologize and takes half a step to pull Ruby away, but Kara puts up a hand.

“No worries. Charlie is our new apprentice. They appear to be the same age.” She gives Sam a faint smile.

Sam lets out an audible breath. “Very well. You must wonder why I’m here.”

“Have you got news of Lena?” Kara asks. She cannot think of another reason why Sam would come to her house. The cramped hallway they’re standing in feels entirely too small in that moment, and her chest tightens.

“I’m afraid no,” Sam says, and her expression turns solemn. “I foolishly thought I might find her here. You haven’t had word of her either, since the men came back? The Luthors were not at church on Sunday, where I meet Lena regularly. I worry.”

Kara’s heart sinks. She had been right to worry, she thinks. “No, I have not seen her, since her brother and father came back. And her … her fiancé, of course.” She clears her throat.

She goes on. “I was there when the ship docked. I saw the gold.”

“Really? There must have been quite the crowd!” Sam says. She quickly glances at Ruby in the kitchen, but the two children are talking among themselves. Her eyes soften for a moment before she turns back to Kara.

Kara doesn’t know much about Sam. Lena had occasionally mentioned her, the two of them would go drink tea together or meet at Sam’s house. All she knows is that Sam’s husband works in California, and that Sam is a seamstress.

Before she can say anything, tell Sam how unwell Lionel had looked, or how self-assured Lena’s brother and fiancé, Alex comes down the stairs. She’s wearing one of Eliza’s old tea gowns, and her hair is hastily tied into a bun that has strands of hair standing out in every direction.

When she sees Sam, her eyes go wide for a moment before she schools her expression back into a neutral one. Nevertheless, Sam and Alex regard each other for a long moment. Then, Alex simply nods and heads into the kitchen. She walks past Ruby and Charlie and keeps preparing their dinner right where Kara had left off.

When Sam looks back to Kara, Kara could swear she sees a faint blush on the woman’s cheeks. It is hard to tell in the dim light, however, and Sam is quick to speak up.

“What do you suggest we do?” she asks, and Kara finds herself confused.

“We do?” she echoes.

“Lena. I worry about her. She has come to enjoy such great freedoms in the men’s absence, and I daren’t imagine that they keep her in the house to be away from us. Now that I know you have no news of her either, I wish to find a way to contact her, to know she is alright.”

Hearing Sam mention the freedoms Lena had enjoyed the past year, it’s Kara’s turn to blush. She looks at her feet. She can hear Alex’ voice in her mind, telling her to let Lena go. It is impossible, of course. And yet, despite her worry, she knows she cannot simply show up at the Luthor house. Lena had forbidden it all those months ago, except the one afternoon in the winter when she led Kara there herself … Kara’s cheeks are burning when she looks back up.

“I worry as well. But I cannot go to the Luthor house. Her brother and her maid have seen me before. You may go, if you wish” she says and bites her lip. “When I went to the harbor to see the ship, I saw the Luthors. Her father looked unwell, even from a distance. I worry about Lena, but I know she will come to me when she can.”

She notices Sam looking at her strangely, and she clears her throat. “Or come to you, of course.”

Sam seems to want to say something, but doesn’t. “Very well. I will wait and see if the Luthors come to church this Sunday. If not, I shall come here again.”

Kara nods. “Agreed.”

Sam gives her a small smile. “You are right, by the way. My house may be closer, but Lena would come knocking on your door first.”

It is said without malice or jealousy, but it leaves Kara stunned nonetheless. Sam calls for Ruby to go home, apologizes to Kara and Alex for causing a fuss, and leaves before Kara has made sense of the statement.

At dinner, Charlie excitedly shows Eliza his progress with his ABCs, though he cannot seem to tear his eyes away from the writing paper where Ruby had spelled her name and drawn a picture of herself holding a baseball glove.

Alex asks about how long Sam has lived in Seattle, since she appears to be around Alex’ age but Alex has never seen her before. Growing up in the city before the Great Fire, they used to both know almost every child their age. But Kara can only shrug, only telling her what she knows from Lena.

She falls asleep that night thinking of Lena, and how desperately she needs to feel her in her arms again.

***

 

Their lunchbreaks become somewhat of a routine. Charlie usually stays with them, and Kara goes to the bakery to bring them sandwiches each day. Taking out money out of the register so nonchalantly still feels strange to her, and on her walk, she tends to grip it tightly, as if it might disappear at any moment.

Kara is dozing off in her chair, half watching Charlie practice his ABCs and drawing people and boats on the paper when a surprised “Oh!” from Alex startles her awake. Her sister is sitting on the other end of the table, hidden behind today’s newspaper.

“What is it, Alex?” she asks and leans forward. Charlie puts his pencil down and eyes Alex with curiosity.

Alex puts the paper on the table and turns it around. “Read!”

“Oh! An article about the Luthors?” Kara asks, but starts reading. Her eyes narrow at first, but then go wide.

“My Goodness! Who would’ve thought he remembered us?” She and Alex stare at each other for a second.

“What’s written in the paper?” Charlie asks, looking between the two of them.

Kara reads: “Lex Luthor has come back from the Yukon territory as a wealthier man than he even was before. ‘Fortune is fickle’, he tells our paper with a charismatic grin. He and his father as well as business partner spent only a little more than a year up North. When asked about the prospectors heading for the Klondike by the hundreds since the arrival of the steamer Portland, he gave the advice to invest in appropriate attire. He mentions that the rubber boots he acquired at Danvers Footwear on Harbor Road prior to his departure have been long-lasting and able to withstand the harsh conditions.”

“He is the rich brother of your friend Lena!” Charlie exclaims, and Kara gives him a sad smile.

“That’s correct, buddy.” She looks back to Alex. “Should we start preparing for an onrush of prospective miners tomorrow?” she asks and grins.

Alex groans. “No. We take four new orders a day the way we agreed to, and no more same-day service. We can barely keep up the way it is.” She leans forward and grabs the paper.

“What are you doing?” Kara asks when Alex gets up.

“I’m cutting out the article, of course.”

Kara hums in response, then takes another page that’s left on the table. “Multiple shops have started selling outfits for the Klondike,” she says as she scans the page with the advertisements.

“I heard the police wants to enforce a minimum of goods a prospective miner has to bring on a ship, because they are worried people are going to starve up there in the winter.” Alex says.

“That’s reasonable. I mean, Winn’s father left with not much more than what he was wearing. I do wonder how far he will get. I do wonder how far any of them will get.” Kara sighs and puts down the paper again.

Charlie looks from the newspaper back to Kara. “I do, too. The white men don’t listen to the land. They only want to take and destroy.”

Kara runs a hand through her loose hair and huffs. “That’s true.”

“Is there an ad for the grocer’s? They were all out of canned goods this morning.” Alex asks from the other side of the room. She cuts out the article and puts it safely away into a drawer.

“Hmm. Let me see.” Kara turns a page and reads through more advertisements. She sees one for Luthor Luxury Imports and stops short. She reads the ad and frowns, wondering how the Luthors plan on continuing their business now. Would they take their sacks of gold and leave again? Or would they look to profit from the influx of people to the city, like the Danvers are doing themselves? She sighs.

Another advertisement catches her eye. “Oh!” She quickly reads it, a smile spreading over her face.

“What is it, Kara?” Alex asks, sitting down on her chair again. “Are candy sticks for sale again?”

“No,” Kara says and looks up. “Look! An announcement for an event at the library. James is holding a photography event next week. One photograph for four cents! I remember him telling me about his new Kodak a while back! We should go, we haven’t had our photograph taken in years!”

Alex, however, does not seem to share her enthusiasm. “I don’t feel like going to the library. You should take Charlie. Have you ever had your photograph taken, Charlie?”

“I haven’t!” Charlie says, eyes widening in excitement.

“Alex.” Kara’s voice is stern. “You cannot avoid the library forever. James asks about you sometimes. He tells me news of Kelly and-“

“I don’t want to hear of it, Kara.”

Kara sighs and crumples the edge of the paper in her hand. Ink stains her fingers, but she makes no move to wipe it off.

“I’ll take you to the library sometime, Charlie. We can get our photograph taken from James and we can borrow a copy of ‘Tom Sawyer’, I’ll read it to you.” She smiles at the boy.

Charlie nods. He quickly glances into Alex’ direction, but then he returns Kara’s smile.

They soon return to work, and in the afternoon, Winn pays them a visit, holding a large cardboard box. “I bring wool socks!” he exclaims and laughs.

Kara puts down her tools and stands up to come to his aid. “Winn! You’re the best! Have you been able to purchase more materials?”

“I have,” Winn nods as he hands her the box. Charlie gets up as well when they open it to inspect the socks. They are plain in color, but either of them doubts that the miners will have much time to worry about fashion in the harsh winter of the North.

Charlie starts taking them out by the pair and arranging them on the shelf for the customers to see when Winn tugs on his arm.

“I heard you’re practicing writing, buddy. I have something for you.”

Charlie’s eyes go wide. He has only met Winn once briefly in the last days, but he had been fascinated when he learned that Winn made toy steam locomotives and ships. Winn had promised to show him his shop one day, however there had not been any time yet.

Winn hands the boy two game boxes. Kara looks over his shoulder in curiosity. One is a box of dominoes. An image from her own childhood flashes before her eyes, her sitting at the table in the parlor, playing the game with her parents. She swallows thickly. Coming to the Danvers household, they used to play card games inside, and baseball outside.

The second box is a game of children’s picture lotto, depicting illustrations of steam ships, locomotives, bicycles and street cars along the letters of the alphabet. Charlie beams.

“Thank you, Winn!” he exclaims, and Winn smiles back at the boy.

“I hope you enjoy them. If you play with Kara, don’t let her trick you!” Winn laughs.

“I don’t trick at board games!” Kara retorts and crosses her arms.

Winn and Alex exchange a glance that makes Winn only laugh more. Kara mumbles something under her breath.

“Say, Kara” Winn starts after a moment, changing the topic. “Have you heard from Lena? I’ve been reading about the Luthors in the paper.”

Kara lets out an audible sigh. “I haven’t”, she says and sits back down on the stool. She tells Winn of Sam’s visit – he remembers her from when she and Lena had bought something at his shop. Alex goes into the kitchen and shows him the newest article she had cut out earlier, and Winn nods, impressed.

When he leaves later, he hugs Kara tightly. “I do hope you hear from her soon. It’s a shame she has to marry the French businessman. I hope they will at least stay in Seattle, though I have my doubts. All that money …”

Kara can only nod and hug her friend back. His sentiment echoes in her mind for the rest of the afternoon, and after dinner she tries to distract herself with a game of dominoes with Charlie. It works only moderately.

***

 

The rest of the week passes in a blur for Kara. There’s work, errands, chores, newspaper articles … everything in their life seems to revolve around the gold. Kara can hardly believe that it has only been a week since she watched the ship dock at the harbor.

The night before, Eliza had asked Charlie something about his room at the boarding house, and the boy had looked solemn. “Miss Jackson says she wants to rent the room to another worker from next month. Someone coming here to work at the docks, who can pay more rent.”

Eliza had exchanged brief looks with Alex and Kara. It had been news to them. They had only heard of this Miss Jackson as a friendly lady who had taken the boy under her wing. Apparently, however, that had only been the case until the boarding house had more prospective renters than available rooms.

“Where would you stay from August?” Eliza had asked tentatively.

Charlie had only looked at his empty plate. “I know a family that lives by the harbor.”

Kara still feels the sting in her chest now that it is the next morning. The tents by the harbor are a sight known to every local, the settlement that Jeremiah had used to call a ‘disgrace’ at the city hall. ‘Ballast island’, where the people who had lived on this land long before the white settlers had arrived are living on the boulders dumped by the ships that carry the riches of their land off to San Francisco. Kara shudders despite the warm rays of the morning sun.

Charlie’s smile when Eliza had suggested he live with them had been bright and hopeful, and it hasn’t left his face since. He’s dashing next to Kara on the trail uphill, humming a melody unfamiliar to Kara’s ears.

The two of them had gone to the creek to bathe and collect berries on Alex’ request to get out of her hair while she does inventory. Charlie has never been up here, and it reminds Kara of when she had brought Lena here over a year earlier. They reach the meadow, and Kara drops her canvas bag onto the tree trunk. She fishes out Jeremiah’s old baseball glove and a ball, and Charlie’s eyes grow wide in excitement.

Kara laughs and throws the ball towards him. “Catch!”

The boy does and runs a few yards away as Kara slips on the glove. The leather is worn, but it fits Kara’s hand just right. She smiles as Charlie attempts his first throw, the ball hitting the ground just before her feet.

“Try again, buddy! You can come closer!” She picks it up and throw it back to him.

Kara had used to play here, on the very same meadow, with Jeremiah just after her adoption. She had been quiet and reserved, the loss of her parents still a fresh memory in her mind. Jeremiah had lured her out of the house with the promise of picking berries and playing baseball that summer, and Alex had joined them on occasion. It had helped her come out of her shell, trust her new family and find joy in her life. Seeing Charlie laugh as his next attempt hit the ground as well, the realization overcomes her that she is somewhat repeating those experiences.

She swallows thickly. She misses Jeremiah, now more than ever. She knows Alex does, too, even if they don’t talk about it.

“Try again, buddy!” she calls as she throws the ball back, willing herself to enjoy their activity and not give in to memories from days long gone.

They take turns throwing and catching until they’ve tired themselves out, sitting on the grass and eating a few of the berries they had picked. Kara feels like she might go back to sleep at once and loathes the work day ahead of them. It’s Charlie who reminds her they have to go back and open the shop on time.

They’re turning left onto Harbor Road when two things happen at once. Firstly, the bells of the small church on Pine Street start chiming, letting them know it’s already 10 o’clock – they’re late. Secondly, Kara notices a horse carriage parked before their shop. Her heart is in her chest in a second, and she starts rushing so suddenly she almost collides with a man walking into the other direction.

The Danvers don’t know anyone who frequents their shop who rides in a carriage. Her first thought is that it’s Lena, even if the possibility is slim. Lena has always come to the shop in as-plain-as-possible clothing, never drawing attention to her status. Then she thinks it might be the police, giving them trouble for employing Charlie. The possibility is just as slim, but her step increases nevertheless. Next to her, Charlie is panting, trying to keep up with her.

When they reach the shop, Kara finds the door open, and she stops so abruptly on the threshold that Charlie bumps into her. For a moment she thinks she might be seeing wrong, and she blinks. But there she is, standing next to Alex, her hairdo and attire unfamiliar, but her kind face still the same.

“… Kelly?”

The two women seem to not have noticed Kara’s and Charlie’s arrival, because they startle and move away from each other. Kelly turns around, her eyes widening when she sees her.

“Kara! It’s been so long!”

Kara mumbles something in response. Disappointment spreads in her chest at not seeing Lena. Seeing Kelly there, however, brings about a whole new line of questions, and looking at Alex she is also unsure if she wants to hear what has brought Kelly back to the city.

As if reading her mind, Alex turns towards her as well. “Kara! It’s good that you two are back. Can you look after the shop? I’d like a minute with Kelly.”

Kara nods. There is a tremble in Alex’ voice that a stranger would overhear, but Kara can only imagine how her sister is feeling in this moment. Her best friend had married and moved to Tacoma almost two years ago, leaving Alex alone and solemn. While Tacoma isn’t far, the Danvers could not afford the streetcar, and Kelly had not been free to visit, either.

“I’m happy to see you both well, and to see your business thriving,” Kelly says to Kara, her voice friendly. “My husband heard of the gold and decided to visit Seattle for a business opportunity.” She laughs. “We arrived two days ago, and I see we are not the only ones. It was quite difficult to find an available room. Fortunately, he is a reasonable man and has no intention of going North.”

Kara only nods once more. “I’m happy to see you well, too, Kelly.” She smiles at the woman.

Once Alex and Kelly have gone upstairs, Kara and Charlie start working. No clients show up, and Kara imagines they must be driven away by the carriage in front of the shop. It doesn’t inconvenience her, however, as it gives her and Charlie more time to work on their existing orders.

Charlie is unusually chatty, and he excitedly talks about their baseball practice from earlier in the morning. “Kara, can we play baseball with Ruby next time? She said she will teach me!”

Kara laughs. “Sure thing, buddy! But we have to wait if Sam comes to visit us again, because I don’t know where they live.”

Charlie sags his shoulders a little, but then smiles. “Alright.”

A minute or so later, he perks up again. “Who is the woman visiting Alex? Is she rich?”

“Her name is Kelly,” Kara starts, then pauses. “She’s the sister of my friend James, from the reading room. She was once Alex’ best friend, but they haven’t seen each other in a long time.”

Charlie furrows his brows. If in concentration of the stitches he is sewing or in thought, Kara isn’t sure. “Like your friend Lena?”

Kara lets out a small huff. “That’s right, buddy.”

Given the fact that Charlie has only known Kara for a week and has never met Lena, she thinks to find it concerning how much he brings her up. However, she realizes it must be because she brings Lena up at every opportunity herself, carefully reading the paper each night for a chance of finding news on the Luthors.

Once again, the comparison between Lena and Kelly makes her pause. But just like the other day during her argument with Alex, she cannot seem to see through it completely. It would be unlikely, to have Alex and Kelly share the same proximity as she and Lena did on occasion. She knows that there are others – she knows of the Parisian dancer Lena so admires, she has read poems – and yet it seems inconceivable to her. She has known Alex and Kelly almost her whole life.

The sound of footsteps on the stairs interrupts her thoughts. She looks up to find Kelly coming into the shop, her hat pulled as low as possible, and a hand covering her face. She rushes towards the front door in quick steps, and Kara can barely hear the ‘Goodbye, Kara’ she murmurs before she climbs into the waiting carriage. After a beat of silence one of the horses lets out a nicker, and the carriage sets in motion, leaving behind only a cloud of dust.

Kara stares through the open door as the dust settles onto the ground. It takes her a moment to realize that Alex had not seen Kelly to the door, and another one for it to strike her as unusual. She gets up and tells Charlie to call if a client comes in, then makes her way through the backroom and up the stairs. A glance at the clock tells her it is almost lunchtime, and that Eliza will be back soon.

She finds Alex sitting on her bed in their shared chamber, head in her hands.

“Alex, what’s wrong?” she whispers, sitting down next to her. The bed squeaks under both their weight.

Alex says nothing, and it’s only then that Kara notices her chest heaving in silent sobs. She puts an arm around her shoulders. It causes Alex to look up in surprise. If it’s because Alex hadn’t heard Kara enter the chamber or because she had hoped, for a split second, that Kelly had come back, Kara isn’t sure.

Alex cries silently, leaning into Kara’s side. Kara keeps her close. She wonders when the last time was that they’ve shared a moment like this. She doesn’t know what happened with Kelly, but she knows that Alex hasn’t been herself since her friend had gotten married.

“What happened, Alex?” Kara attempts again, but Alex only shakes her head in response.

Kara sighs, but stays where she is, letting her sister know she will listen if she wishes to talk. She doesn’t know how long they sit there, but a while later Charlie calls for Kara as a customer seems to have entered the shop.

“Go” Alex whispers, her voice hoarse.

“Will you be alright?” Kara asks as she gets up.

Alex nods. “I will.”

Kara squeezes her shoulder, then rushes downstairs to help Charlie. Eliza comes back shortly after, and when Kara tells her of Kelly’s visit, there is more concern than surprise on her adoptive mother’s face. Eliza spends some time with Alex upstairs, but Alex does not join them for work, remaining in their chamber until dinner.

She still does not talk of Kelly or what had happened, and it makes Kara’s concern grow even more. Charlie, when he leaves, gives Alex a tight hug, and it brings a small smile to her face.

Notes for the Chapter:

thank you for reading, and the kudos and comments! <3

6. Chapter 6

1896/1897

 

Lena doesn’t come to the harbor often. During the summer, it’s busy and smelly, and ‘no place for a lady to lounge’, as the ship workers like to remind her. As fall advances, however, she sometimes finds herself standing at the docks on her way back from Kara’s house, staring at the mist above the water.

She has no reason to be lonely, she tells herself. She spends her time either at Kara’s or Sam’s house, and she’s grateful to have found true friendship so far away from the place she used to call home. A year ago, when she had only just met Sam, she had still found herself longing for New York City and the superficial conversations with the daughters of her father’s business partners.

And yet it had been a strange feeling to wake up on her birthday a few weeks ago and not have her father and brother there. Lillian had hugged her briefly, not meeting her eyes when she congratulated her. Eve had made a pie and offered to play boardgames in the sitting room. Lena had appreciated the effort but chosen to eat the pie alone in her chamber until Sam had come over and persuaded her to a round of cards.

The kiss Kara had pressed to her cheek and the mumbled ‘happy birthday, Lena’ the next day had made her eyes swell with tears that she hid, and she had clung to Kara’s embrace like a lifeline.

She doesn’t come to the harbor often, but one early afternoon she and Sam stop by the dock the ship that took her father and brother to Alaska departed from, and Lena can’t help a deep sigh from escaping.

“Are you quite alright?” Sam asks.

“Each time I come here, it reminds me of my brother and father. I miss them.”

Sam looks at her for a long moment. “I understand. Have you got any word from them recently?”

Lena shakes her head. “No, the last we heard was that they were headed for the Yukon. But the Yukon river will have long frozen over by now, so we do not expect to hear from them until the spring.”

Sam only hums in response, and they look out at the water in silence for a while.

“Do you ever look out at the water and wish your husband was on one of the ships arriving?” Lena asks after a minute.

Sam takes in a sharp breath and closes her eyes. Lena thinks to apologize for her insensitive question, but something about Sam’s reaction makes her pause and look at her friend. Sam turns around, her cheeks reddened.

“Lena, I think it is time I told you the truth.”

Lena’s eyes go wide. “The truth?”

“I am not married.”

Lena thinks she has misheard, but the intent way that Sam stares at her lets her know her friend is serious. She swallows.

“But Ruby …?”

Sam laughs. “You do not need to be married to conceive a child.”

They look at each other for a moment. Lena feels at a loss for words. She grips her purse tighter, needing something to hold onto. She can almost hear her mother’s disapproving voice in her head, telling Lena to stay away from shameful women like Sam.

“How …?” She clears her throat. “So Ruby’s father is not your husband, is that why he is away in California?”

Sam laughs again, but it sounds sad this time. “I don’t know where Ruby’s father is. I don’t know anyone in California, either. I made up a lie.”

Lena lets out a shaky breath, processing the information. She looks around them to make sure nobody can overhear their conversation, but the docks are deserted except for two native men standing several yards away. A sailor stumbles out of one of the waterfront saloons, but Lena doubts he pays them much attention.

“I’m sorry I lied to you, Lena” Sam continues before Lena has a chance to say anything. “But it was the only way for me to be safe. When I first met you, I didn’t know what you would think. Now I have known for a while that you are not the same as your family, and I can trust you with my secret. But for everyone else I meet, Ruby and I have to keep up this façade.” Sam sighs.

Lena’s eyes meet Sam’s. “How much of what I know about you is the truth?”

Sam closes her eyes, and Lena braces herself to hear that it has all been a lie. “Is your name really Samantha Arias?” The question leaves her mouth before she can think better of it.

“Yes. I used a different name before I came to Seattle, but I knew I wanted to stay here, and it was easier to use my real name.” Sam answers so nonchalantly Lena realizes she must have anticipated the question.

Sam takes a step towards Lena and touches her arm in a friendly gesture. “It’s quite cold to stand around. Would you like to walk while I tell you my story?”

As if on cue, Lena shivers as a cold breeze reaches them from the water. She nods and takes a last look at the small waves before they turn around and start walking. The unexpected turn the afternoon has taken has made her forget all about her longing to see her brother and father.

Sam walks in front of her for a while and Lena begins to wonder if she has changed her mind, if she is trying to rush home and shut her out when Sam turns around again. There are tears in her eyes, and Lena feels her chest tighten at the sight.

“I’ve been lying for so long that it feels strange to admit the truth out loud,” she says quietly and stops to let Lena catch up.

“You’re always so kind, Lena. When we baked pie together in the spring and you asked Ruby about her letter, I almost told you. But I was too scared. Your concern for Ruby was so genuine because you thought it was something you had in common with her – to miss family. And Ruby loves you so much, you’re like a cousin or an older sister to her. We don’t have any other family. And you’re always so kind, and I never doubted your kindness, but I was so scared …” her voice trails off, and she wipes her eyes with the sleeve of her jacket.

Lena steps forward to hug her friend. “It’s alright, Sam. You are a good person. I trust that, no matter what you are going to tell me. I will still be your friend. I just want to know the truth, now that you are not who I thought you were …”

Sam looks at her as if she wants to say something, but only nods. She tugs at Lena’s arm, and they keep walking. They’re on a quiet side street to the north-east of the harbor. A horse-drawn carriage drives past them, and once the dust settles, they’re alone.

“I was born in New York and I was a dancer, that much is true. But it was not the kind of dancing you might have had in mind. I danced in small, rowdy bars with little clothing, and I lied about my age. My stage-name was Reign, but in that business, nobody cares to ask your real name or age. I had run away from home, and I made enough money to live in a clean room at a boarding house.” Sam swallows thickly.

Lena listens as they walk slowly. She cannot imagine herself living such a life of uncertainty and trouble. She admires her friend all the more for it.

“I began having an affair with one of the patrons who frequented the bar I performed in on Saturdays. He was a fairly successful salesman, but he was all new money. The son of a former slave and an Irish immigrant. I genuinely admired him.” Sam laughs. “I was so young, much younger than you are now, Lena. I thought I loved him.”

“What happened?” Lena asks, feeling breathless.

“He broke off our affair. It turned out that he had a woman he wanted to propose to. I had thought him a bachelor, but then again, I had never asked. I was used to not asking questions, to only take what was offered to me. I was so naïve, but I thought I had figured out how the world works.” She laughs again.

“The last time I lay with him, I felt so jealous of the woman he would marry. After he had fallen asleep, I searched his things. I’m not proud of this. But I found a small stack of money, and the ring. I took it all and left. When I went home and counted the money, it was much more than I had thought. I panicked, thinking he would find me at my boarding house somehow. So I packed the few things I owned and checked into a hotel. It was so much money, Lena.”

They stop at an intersection, but Lena has no eye for their surroundings. Her gaze is fixed on Sam, eagerly waiting for her to continue. Sam starts walking faster as if it helps her get the words out.

“I never showed up to a performance again. I spent a week or so at the hotel, enjoying the breakfast that was brought to my room each morning. It was like a dream. Until one morning I couldn’t hold in the breakfast. Then the next morning, too, and I spent all day in bed. Then I realized that my monthlies were long overdue.”

Lena takes in a sharp breath, as if she had not anticipated this was where the story was heading. Ruby had turned 11 this year, after all. Nevertheless, she is shocked by the circumstances that Sam is revealing.

“I panicked again, and the next day I packed my belongings again and headed for the railway station. I just wanted to get away. My belly hadn’t grown yet, but I couldn’t hide my sickness. It never once crossed my mind to … to get rid of the child growing within me. Maybe it was part of my naivety. I still thought I loved him. At the same time, all I thought to do was get away.” She sighs.

“I bought a passenger ticket for the first train that would get me out of New York. It took me to St. Louis. I had never even left the city before. Thinking back to that day, all I remember is a scared, lonely girl who made too many bad decisions in too short a time.”

Lena is too absorbed in Sam’s story to be able to react.

“I always enjoyed reminiscing about New York with you,” Sam continues. “But as you see, the New York that you remember, and the New York that I remember are two different worlds. Once I left, I never thought about going back.”

“My New York was …” Lena starts, but trails off. She cannot come up with an adequate word. A sudden memory comes to her mind. A younger Lena sitting in the house each May 1st, Lillian forbidding her from going out and getting caught up in the masses that would move house all on the same day.

When she mentions it, Sam lets out a low laugh. “Oh, I remember that! I certainly do not miss it. It was utter chaos each year.”

Her face turns serious again, and she continues. “When I was on the train, I remembered I still had the engagement ring in my bag. I thought about selling it, but then I had a better idea.” She holds up her hand.

“I put it on and made up a story about my husband’s whereabouts. I found a boarding house in St. Louis, where I would eventually give birth to Ruby. But the first few months I earned a living by dancing again, just in a wider costume to hide my belly. When I couldn’t move anymore, the sweet old lady that ran the boarding house taught me how to use a sewing machine. Once Ruby was born, she taught me how to take care of a baby. I had my made-up backstory on the tip of my tongue at all times, but she never asked. I think she was lonely, too.”

“Why did you leave St. Louis?” Lena asks.

Sam sighs. They turn right on a street corner. Lena can feel the chill of the afternoon getting to her as the sun has started going down, but she cannot bring herself to ask Sam to stop or walk to a streetcar stop.

“The old lady passed away, and the new boarding house owner disliked me and Ruby. Ruby was four by that time, and quite a handful.” She laughs. “She still is.”

Lena smiles.

“I could have searched for a different boarding house in St. Louis, the city was growing each year. But I saw it as a sign to move on. So, we boarded a train to Chicago.” Sam wraps an arm around her torso. If it’s to protect herself from the chill or the memories, Lena isn’t sure.

“We stayed for less than three years. You surely remember reading about the World’s Fair in the papers. People came to Chicago from all over the world, the city was bustling with them. Most importantly, many businesspeople came from New York. I was working as a seamstress in a small shop downtown. I feared that Ruby’s father or other men who frequented the bars I used to dance at would find me. I had committed a robbery, after all, and while there was no way he knew he had fathered a child, I couldn’t be sure that I wasn’t a wanted person in New York. I liked the city, but once the Fair started, I couldn’t find peace anymore. I didn’t want to take Ruby away from the other children she had become friends with, but we had to move. And this time I decided to go as far as I possibly could.” She laughs.

“To Seattle.” Lena whispers.

Sam nods. “I had heard much about the city, about the rebuilding after the Great Fire. I stayed at a boarding house at first, but it became more and more of a hassle with Ruby. The other women who lived there called her a little untamed rascal, and they were suspicious about the absence of my husband. I had come up with the California story on the train to Seattle, but now that people asked questions, I realized I did not want to tell it each time we sat together. I still had some savings, and we moved to the house.” She sighs again.

“It was good for Ruby and me.” She continues with a small smile. “We were alone with each other for the first time in Ruby’s life. I had a quiet place of my own to work and rest when I chose to. Ruby could sing and dance and scream as much as she wanted to – we had no neighbors for the first year. Even when the recession started and work gradually became less, I was happy. I am happy, here.”

Sam turns towards Lena and smiles. They have stopped walking, and Lena reaches out towards her friend for a hug. She has never been someone to initiate physical contact, and maybe it’s Kara’s influence, but she feels the need to provide comfort to Sam. Sam hugs Lena tightly, and when they separate, she has tears in her eyes.

“Thank you, Lena. For not walking away.”

Lena feels her chest tighten at the thought of her friend thinking she might quit her. “Of course. I will always be your friend, Sam. You are incredibly brave.”

She smiles. “And I am happy to find out that Ruby is part Irish! So I am somewhat of a sister to her.”

Sam laughs as a tear rolls down her cheek. “You truly are.” She wipes at her eyes with the sleeve of her jacket again.

“It’s getting rather chilly. Why don’t we walk towards the nearest streetcar stop and see if it takes us home?” she suggests.

Lena nods. When she looks around, she sees that the streets are busier again where they are and realizes they must have turned West at one point, back towards downtown. She has no recollection how far they have walked, but now that they have paused, her feet are aching.

They walk in silence until Lena thinks of a question. “Does your mother know about Ruby?”

Sam tilts her head as if she had been expecting the question. “I don’t know,” she says, quietly. “During our last Christmas in St. Louis, I sent her a letter with Ruby’s picture in it. But I had no way of knowing if she still lived in the house we had lived in when I was 15. And I used a false sender address so she would not find me should she decide to look for me. I have no way of knowing if the letter reached her or not.” Her expression turns solemn.

“I wonder about her the older I get; the older Ruby gets. Having a daughter of my own, it has changed my perspective. I could never live with myself if Ruby ran away from me. I never want to be a mother so terrible that her child chooses to run away.”

Lena swallows thickly, remembering a conversation they once had about Ruby playing baseball with her friends. “You’re a fantastic mother. And Ruby is a great daughter who will grow up to be a strong woman who does not let anyone dictate what she can and can’t do. The world needs more children like Ruby, and more mothers like you.”

Sam stops and looks at Lena with an unreadable expression. Out of the corner of her eye Lena can see a group of men staring at them, but she pays them no mind. “I’m so thankful I’ve met you, Lena. For Ruby, and for myself.”

The next time that Lena visits Sam’s house, it all feels different. Sam isn’t wearing her ring, and when Ruby comes tumbling down the stairs and hugs her, she whispers into Lena’s ear: “Mommy said you know our secret now. I have always wanted to tell you about my friend Carl in Chicago.”

Lena doesn’t go back to the harbor all winter.

 

***

 

The winter, Lena finds, brings about a change of habits to the Luthor mansion. Lillian moves her business work from the sitting room to Lionel’s study. Not consciously, not in one day, but one day Lena notices the absence of the cluttered paper stacks on the table, and realizes her mother uses the upstairs room now.

When she asks her about it at dinner one evening, Lillian gives a shrug. “I know that Lionel forbade we use it, but the study warms up better than any other chamber in the house, and I know he would not mind me using it for the winter months.”

But there are other changes, as well. When before Lena had been under the watchful eye of either Eve or Lillian at all times, now Lillian leaves her alone in the house more frequently, taking Eve on errands with her throughout the city.

It makes Lena grow bolder, in turn, and instead of sneaking in and out of the study to quickly borrow a book and read it in her own chamber, she uses their absence to lounge in the leather chair and read the daily papers the same way Lex would before.

Standing in the study now, however, heart pounding in her chest she is sure the Smith family must hear it on the next property, she wonders if she has been too bold. Kara is standing in front of the shelf, taking in the vast collection of books that Lena’s father prides himself on.

Lena has been watching her take a closer look, run her fingers over the backs of books, even taking some out and flipping through the pages in wonder for over a quarter of an hour now. It is almost as if Kara has forgotten Lena’s presence at all, and yet Lena finds herself not caring. She studies the way Kara’s eyes light up and the way her nose crinkles, the way she moves so carefully within the room.

She walks towards Kara and looks over her shoulder. Kara turns slightly, and all at once their faces are close. Lena’s heart pounds even faster, if now for a different reason.

“This library is quite impressive,” Kara whispers. “I can see why you enjoy spending your time here. It is so different from the public library, or the books my parents used to own.”

At the mention of Kara’s parents, Lena perks up. Kara usually hesitates to mention them. “What kind of books did your parents own?”, she asks tentatively.

“A lot of fictional novels and essay collections. They would read in their downtime, for leisure. It seems like your father and your brother only read for business,” Kara says. “I wouldn’t find a copy of Frankenstein here, would I?” she adds and grins.

Lena laughs. “No, I suppose you wouldn’t.”

Kara hums and grabs for a book that catches her interest. Lena squints, trying to read the cover when she realizes the language is not English. Kara, however, does not seem to pay it any mind and opens it with another enthusiastic hum. A world map appears in front of their eyes, the continents split up into two circles that take up a double page. Kara gently traces the coastline of the Pacific Northwest with her finger.

“Kara?” Lena asks after a minute.

Kara hums in response.

“Is that German?”

The book shuts with a loud thud, the sound lingering in the quiet of the room. Kara clears her throat.

“… yes.”

Lena looks at the title again. She can guess the meaning of the words, if barely. Weltatlas – die neue Geographie der westlichen und östlichen Hemisphäre. She has no idea why the book is in her family’s possession, none of them have ever learned German as far as she is aware.

Kara puts the book back onto the shelf, and Lena notices that her hands are trembling.

“Are you alright?” she asks quietly.

Kara turns around and nods, but the way her eyes dart around to look anywhere but Lena’s face betrays her. “I simply … was just reminded of my parents.”

Lena’s eyes go wide. “Your parents?” Then understanding dawns on her. “Oh …”

“I cannot see writing in my parent’s native language and not be reminded of them … even if I think I have largely forgotten how to speak it.” Kara whispers.

She looks up to meet Lena’s gaze. Her face is only inches apart from Lena’s, and the proximity, combined with the glow of the electric light, leaves nowhere to hide the vulnerability in her features.

“It is still too painful for me to speak of them, even with Alex and Eliza. Forgive me, Lena, for not telling you sooner.”

Lena reaches out to touch her arm. Kara is wearing her usual men’s pullover over her blouse, a gift from Winn as Lena had learned, and the fabric is raspy under her fingertips. “It is alright, Kara. I understand.”

She is curious. There has not been a day since she has met Kara that she hasn’t been curious about her, but she knows not to push her friend. Sam’s confession about her past all those weeks ago comes to her mind. It makes her wonder how much more there really is to the people around her, how much goes untold and remains unknown.

“My legal name is Zoller.” Kara blurts out, and it snaps Lena out of her thoughts.

“I started using the Danvers name after my adoption because it was easier, but it is not my name on paper. I chose not to change it, to honor my parents.” Kara speaks quickly, as if she fears that Lena might turn around and leave the room if she stops.

Lena gently squeezes her arm to remind her that she is there and will listen to her talk if that’s what Kara needs.

“They came to America as a young couple before I was born, my father always spoke highly of the business opportunities here.” Kara continues and takes a deep breath.

It’s a family history that many, if not most of the people Lena knows share, but she listens with attention to not miss a word that Kara utters.

“They stayed in New York for a while but eventually came here by railroad and settled here. Not unlike your family.” Kara gives her a sad smile.

“I rescued some books from … from the house, and I read them occasionally, so I do not forget them, or the German language. But I have not spoken it since, and I likely never will again.” Her voice trails off at the end, and she looks away from Lena.

Lena runs her hand down Kara’s arm and reaches for her hand, in hopes it provides some comfort. Kara’s hand is warm and soft, and Kara runs her thumb over Lena’s palm in response. It makes Lena blush, and she has half a mind to retract her hand when Kara looks up at her with a small smile. There is a faint blush on her cheeks that matches Lena’s.

Neither of them speaks, Lena processing the new information. The more she learns about Kara, the more she cannot help but admire her strength and resilience. She wishes she had the words to express her gratitude for Kara sharing more about her parents’ past.

“I would like to see more of the upstairs.” Kara requests after a moment.

Lena startles slightly, having been lost in the proximity to Kara once more. “Of course,” she says and clears her throat.

They leave the study, Lena making sure all the books are in place and the door is shut properly to not raise suspicion in case her mother enters it upon her return. The house is unusually quiet, and it reminds her of the first few weeks after her father’s and brother’s departure. When she thinks of them, she often forgets to think about Benoit – it should make her ashamed, and yet she cannot bring herself to care. And yet now, as she leads Kara to her own chamber, she feels her cheeks growing hot with abashment.

“This is my private chamber,” Lena announces as she opens the door, and Kara’s eyes go wide in wonder once they step inside.

Lena looks around as if seeing it for the first time herself. The chamber is more spacious than the whole of the upstairs of the Danvers household, and each perfume bottle on her vanity more expensive than the shoes Alex and Kara manufacture daily. A variety of books and magazines clutters every surface imaginable. Regret forms in Lena’s stomach, but before she can speak up, Kara turns around to her.

“It is quite lovely. However-“ Lena’s heart jumps into her throat.

Kara’s eyes have a teasing glint in them. “- not as tidy as one would expect from a young, respectable lady.” She shakes a finger.

Lena takes a moment to process the jest, then laughs in relief. “Did my mother ask you to say that?”

“Oh yes, I saw her when I left the beauty salon a fortnight ago, I must have forgotten to mention it!” Kara says it with a straight face, but the two of them start to chuckle at once.

“I have never once complained about the clothing strewn across your room!” Lena exclaims and pushes at Kara’s arm.

“No, but Alex certainly complains about it for two!” Kara laughs again and to Lena’s surprise, catches her arm and pulls Lena towards her.

Lena’s breath hitches in her throat. Suddenly, they’re close again, and Kara’s hand finds posture on her lower back. Kara has gone quiet too, and when Lena looks up finds her studying her face. Lena can feel her hands trembling, so she puts them on Kara’s arms to steady them.

Just like before, she can feel Kara’s warmth through the raspy fabric of the pullover, and without thinking she runs her hands up until she is touching her biceps. Lena gasps a little at the muscle she finds there, and the way Kara breathes out audibly reminds her that this is not just another one of her late-night imaginations, but instead very real. She blushes deeply, but when she looks at Kara’s face, she finds that the blonde has her eyes closed. Feeling bold, she lets her thumbs graze Kara’s arms.

“Did you have this in mind when you asked me to visit?” Kara whispers.

Lena’s blush deepens, but Kara’s eyes remain closed. Lena studies her face, the swing of her eyebrows, the scar above one of them, the light hairs on her cheeks. When Kara opens her eyes unexpectedly, all air leaves Lena’s lungs. They’re so close, and she has no idea what to do with herself. A blush spreads over Kara’s cheeks, letting her know she is not the only one affected by the proximity.

She moves her hand from Kara’s arm to her shoulder, the vane of her neck until she caresses her cheek. Kara’s blush deepens further, and Lena can see her swallow thickly. Kara’s eyes flicker down to Lena’s lips and back up in an instant so quick that she almost misses it. But she sees it, and she releases a shaky breath.

It would be so easy to simply close the last few inches between them and touch Kara’s lips with her own. Once she thinks about it, she is overcome with desire to do so. Her fingers halt, and Kara catches them with her own. She looks at Lena with expectation.

“Do you mind giving in, just this once?” Kara whispers, and it sends goosebumps down Lena’s arms.

“I fear I might not be able to withhold myself,” Lena whispers back, her voice quivering.

Kara takes in a sharp breath, her pupils dilating. There is an intensity burning behind her eyes unlike anything Lena has ever seen before, as if Kara is looking directly into her very soul. “Would that be such a bad thing to fear?”

Before Lena has an answer, Kara’s lips are touching her own. Her world narrows down to this one sensation, this overwhelming feeling that spreads through her body like electricity. Kara’s lips are soft, and when Kara pulls away, she chases them, reconnecting their kiss. Kara sighs against her lips and it makes Lena feel warm inside.

The kiss is slow and tentative. There is no rush to it, a simple exploration of each other’s mouths. Kara’s hands are firm on Lena’s back, and Lena leans into the touch. She can feel heat pool low in her belly, the illicit beginning of a desire she has never explored beyond the hidden trails of her imagination. A trail that Kara now has dangerously opened up before her.

She can feel abashment creep up her neck once more, the same sensation she had when she opened the door to her chamber for Kara just a few minutes prior. It’s what makes her pull away from Kara’s lips, breathless and with clammy hands.

If Kara can sense her inner turmoil, she refrains from inquiring about it. Instead, she caresses Lena’s cheek with her thumb and an abashed smile before pulling her in for a hug. Lena reciprocates, clinging to Kara, letting herself be engulfed in her warmth, finding comfort in her embrace. Around them, the house is quiet save for Lena’s heartbeat pounding away in her chest.

 

***

 

„Penny for your thoughts?“ Lena nudges Kara, an amused expression on her face. They’re sitting underneath the oak tree, on the meadow not far from the creek. The air is alive with the buzz of insects and the chirping of birds, but no other people are in sight.

Kara is sitting next to her but hasn’t paid any attention to the book she had been reading earlier. Lena had noticed a while ago, finding Kara staring across the fields into the direction of the city downhill. Each time she had looked up, Kara still hadn’t turned a page, her fingers resting motionlessly on the paper.

Kara startles, but instead of laughing, she bites her lip and looks down. “Sorry”, she mumbles and starts to read, but it is evident from the way her eyes scan the page that she is not taking any of it in.

Lena frowns. “Are you alright?”

She reaches out and lets her fingers rest on Kara’s arm. Kara’s sleeves are rolled up in the un-ladylike fashion that Lena finds so endearing, and as always Lena feels a tingle when she touches her exposed skin. She doesn’t let herself dwell on it, this time, however.

Kara turns towards her. There is a sadness in her eyes that Lena has seen before. Her heart sinks. Kara shifts slightly, leaning a little more into Lena’s space. She takes another look at the buildings, the streets and the harbor visible from their spot and sighs.

“Today would be my mother’s birthday,” Kara whispers.

Lena’s heart sinks impossibly deeper. “Oh, Kara …”

She takes her hand in her own, a gesture of comfort now familiar to the both of them. She runs her thumb over the palm of Kara’s hand, and Kara hums in response.

They sit in silence for a minute until Kara shifts once more. She puts her book away and moves closer to Lena, resting her head on her shoulder and draping her arms around her torso. Lena feels her heartbeat quicken. It has become a familiar position for them to be in over the spring. There is no one around to see them, so Lena presses a tentative kiss to the top of Kara’s head.

After their kiss in Lena’s chamber in the winter, their friendship had deepened. Kara had always been showering Lena with affection, but now their interactions are different. They sit closely together at all times, holding each other’s hand when they’re alone in a room.

Just this once, Kara had said before their kiss, and true to her word Lena had not given into the urge to press her lips to Kara’s for a second time for all the rest of winter and all of spring. Now that summer is near, her resolve hangs on a thread. However, neither of them has brought up their kiss again, and so Lena holds herself back and simply enjoys the comfort Kara’s proximity always brings her.

“I was here when they died.”

Kara speaks so suddenly that Lena’s heart skips a beat.

“I mean, I was sitting under this tree, overlooking the city that was not as big back then as it is now, and I was reading a book. It was a warm day in June, almost eight years ago. I came here often because I liked the quiet. You see, there was only one ranch out here at the time, and the cattle used to graze where there is now the horse stable of one of the families who moved here after …” she trails off before she clears her throat. “… after that day. I was friends with the farmer’s boy who was my age. I do not know what became of him, sadly.”

Her words come in waves, and her voice is quiet. Lena exhales, realizing she had been holding her breath while Kara had spoken. Kara has taken her to the oak tree once or twice before, on their return from the creek, and she feels foolish for not having known of its importance to Kara before.

“What happened that day?” She asks softly.

Kara’s breath hitches. “I saw smoke.”

Lena inhales sharply. It hits her like an ox-cart full of bricks. The Great Fire. It had happened long before she moved here, but she remembers reading about it in the papers at the time. She had however, been unaware that there had been casualties.

“It was only them. They were the only ones who died that day,” Kara adds as if reading her mind. She lets out a quiet sob, and Lena pulls her closer.

“I ran back downhill as fast as I could. The closer I got, the more it was evident how close to our house the fire was. There was smoke in the streets, you couldn’t see very far, and a policeman told me to turn around. I felt so helpless. The fire spread so quickly. Seattle was a lumber town back then, you see, everything was made out of wood, all the buildings that they replaced with brick. We lived above our small shop on Spring Street. It was next to a liquor store, and the liquor store exploded like fireworks.” Her words are rushed now, and another sob escapes her throat.

Lena feels her chest tighten she fears she might not be able to breathe. A tear rolls down Kara’s cheek, getting soaked up by the fabric of Lena’s blouse. Lena holds Kara even closer.

“I’m sorry,” she whispers into her ear.

Kara grips her hand tighter in response. They sit like this for a while. Kara’s sobs grow quieter as she calms down, but she never lets go of Lena’s hand. Her hair tingles on Lena’s cheeks, but Lena stays still. In the distance they can hear a man shouting and a horse stomping, but other than that they remain alone on the meadow underneath the tree.

“It’s all just so unfair, how your life can change in an instant. Why me? I was having a peaceful day, reading my book under the tree, and someone I’ve never met trips over a pot of glue, and it changes my life forever.”

Another silent tear rolls down her cheek. “Now all I have left are two charred photographs, some books, and some plates and teacups from the shop.”

Lena reaches out to wipe the tear away with her thumb. “I understand. What it feels like when your life changes so suddenly.” She clears her throat.

“I watched my mother drown when I was four.”

Kara’s eyes go wide, and she shifts so she can look at Lena. Lena is unsure if sharing her own pain will help Kara, but Kara eases that worry when she asks, “Do you remember your birth parents?”

Lena hesitates. Her and Kara have gradually shared more about their families over the past year that they’ve known each other, but it doesn’t make it any easier for Lena to bring her memories back to the surface. Especially since Lillian had forbidden her from ever talking about her life in Ireland the moment she had crossed the threshold into the Luthor house in New York all those years ago.

“I never met my father. My clearest memory of my mother is her standing in the kitchen at our holiday house by the lake, singing songs to me.” She pauses. “I was so young when it happened, and I couldn’t understand why she wasn’t coming back, why I was being taken to this big house in the city where I had to share a room with seven other children I had never met.”

Kara says nothing, but she presses a kiss to Lena’s fingers. The sensation sends a tingle down Lena’s spine, and she swallows. She takes a moment to gather her thoughts before she continues.

“And when I was adopted, I didn’t understand what it meant when this unfamiliar woman told me, through gritted teeth, she would be my mother from now on, when she was nothing like my mother at all. Lionel treated me nicely, but he never spent much time with me. Only Lex made me feel welcome, he began teaching me chess before I could even spell my name.” She lets out a low laugh at the memory.

“I’m glad that you had him. I don’t know what I would have done without Alex after my adoption.” Kara offers, and Lena nods before her expression turns solemn again.

“As I got older, I often wondered why the Luthors chose me. I still do. What could have possibly prompted them to visit the particular orphanage in Dublin that I was living in, and to adopt me? Why me? There were so many other girls and boys. Children that had spent most of their lives in the orphanage, not only a couple of months. Didn’t they deserve to be adopted by a rich family more than me?”

“Oh, Lena-“ Kara says and tightens her grip on her hand.

“These things are out of our control. You deserved to be safe and sound as much as the other children in the orphanage. When I lost my parents and my house, I went from having my own chamber to sharing a small room with Alex. But the Danvers were loving and accepting of me, so all I could do was count my blessings and be thankful to be alive, and that I could finish school. These things are out of our control, and you mustn’t agonize over them so much.”

Lena bites her lip. “I know … forgive me, for distracting from your talk about your parents. It wasn’t my intention to lead away from-”

“Lena, it is alright.” Kara interrupts her. She catches Lena’s gaze, and Lena gets lost for a moment in how soft and blue they are. Their faces are close, suddenly, and she swallows thickly.

“We may not have had the same path in life, but I have never met anyone who has understood me better than you, Lena. Please know that. I take great comfort in knowing you, in having you by my side.” Kara whispers, eyes darting across Lena’s face.

Lena feels her heartbeat pick up. Once more, she finds herself at a loss for meaningful words, despite her chest being tight with emotion. She blurts out a question, picking up on something Kara had mentioned.

“Where did you go to school?” She feels foolish once the question leaves her mouth, feels like Kara deserves to hear how much she means to Lena in turn, but Kara’s lips curl into a smile.

“The Jewish grammar school near the public market.”

Lena’s eyes widen. She has walked past the building before. It’s an esteemed school, as far as she knows. It once again hits her how much more there is to Kara than the girl lets on.

“How …?” she starts, but she closes her mouth when she realizes she has no clue how to finish the question.

Kara smiles sadly. “How did a girl whose parents owned a successful chinaware business downtown and who studied French and Hebrew at school end up being a poor shoemaker?” she finishes the question for Lena, but there is no malice in her voice.

“As I told you, I have agonized over my fate many times over the years. But living in pity for myself was not the answer. The world is unjust, but I had a loving adoptive family, and I wanted to be as happy as I could with them. My parents were good friends of Eliza and Jeremiah, I know they would want me to be happy with them.”

“You’re so strong and brave,” Lena whispers. “I love that about you.”

Kara bites her lip and looks down at their entwined hands. When she looks back up, there is an intensity burning in her eyes that Lena has only seen once before. Her breath hitches in her throat.

“I know we said ‘just this once’” Kara says with a hoarse voice, “but would you mind giving in once more?”

All the air seems to have left Lena’s lungs. Her eyes dart down towards Kara’s lips, then back up again. “Here?” she croaks out.

“There’s no one around to see.”

There is a pause, and neither of them moves. Then, Kara lets out an unexpected chuckle. “Now that I told you I learned French at school, is this a bad time to admit I read the magazine article about your Parisian dancer?”

Lena’s mind goes blank for a moment before she catches up to Kara’s words. “I …” She feels crimson creep up her neck.

“It made me understand why you were curious about her. It made me see you differently, you know.” Kara pauses. Her eyes are glistening. “It made me curious about you.”

Lena barely processes the meaning behind what Kara has said before she leans forward and crashes their lips together. Kara lets out a small yelp but quickly shifts, one hand landing on Lena’s hip, her lips chasing Lena’s.

The kiss is different than the first. Where the first one in Lena’s chamber in the winter had been slow and tentative, this one is more urgent, and Lena can taste the salt on Kara’s sun-tanned skin.

She cannot think of anything more scandalous than two young women kissing in an open field, but at the same time she cannot bring herself to pull away from Kara. The taste of Kara’s lips is enticing. Their resolution of just this once echoes somewhere in the back of her mind, like the sound of a bell fading in the quiet of the morning, only to be swallowed by the noises of the city.

She lets out a small whimper when Kara opens her mouth and their tongues meet, and her entire body flushes. They deepen the kiss, and Kara shifts again and presses her chest against Lena’s. Lena reaches out and places her palm against Kara’s neck, pulling her ever closer.

When they finally separate, Kara flops back onto the grass, laughing as her loose hair frames her face. Lena stares at her for a moment in wonder before she smiles, lowering herself so that her head can rest on Kara’s chest. Their books are discarded next to them on the grass, forgotten about as the hours of the afternoon tick by, the two of them wrapped up in each other’s presence.

7. Chapter 7

1897

 

Kara awakes with a start, sitting up in bed. The chamber is dark save for some moonlight shining in through the window. She puts a hand over her chest, willing her wildly beating heart to calm down. Simply another nightmare, she tells herself.

Nevertheless, she daren’t close her eyes again. If she does, she might see the flames burning behind her eyelids, might feel as if the thick smoke suffocates her. She takes a deep breath. The rustling of sheets from the other side of the chamber makes her turn her head despite the darkness.

“Kara? Are you quite alright?” Alex voice carries quietly over to her.

Kara hums. “Yes. Simply a nightmare.” She rubs her temple.

“I’m sorry.” Alex says, and Kara can hear her sister sit up in bed.

“’s alright. I’ve not had one in a while.” Kara sighs. “Why aren’t you sleeping? It must be quite late.”

“I can’t sleep,” Alex replies and yawns. “I simply … I can’t seem to stop thinking of Kelly.”

“Will you tell me why she left so urgently today?”

There’s a beat of silence, then a sigh. “I … she asked me why I had never returned her letters when she moved away. I told her I tried to forget her, because she had gotten married. I simply …”

Kara shifts on her bed, turning towards Alex side of the chamber despite only being able to make out her silhouette. “Why would you try to forget her?”

She has an inkling, and yet it seems like a penny at the bottom of a well. It’s reflecting back at her, but still out of reach. Alex says nothing. Kara tries to imagine Lena moving away now that her fiancé has returned. She likes to think she would try and send her as many letters as possible, to keep Lena within reach if only in her thoughts.

Alex speaks up again, and Kara can hear the tremble in her voice. “It broke my heart. Losing Kelly broke my heart, after everything I had already lost. I thought if I tried to forget her, I could ease my pain, but I … I lo-“ she ends abruptly.

“You loved her,” Kara whispers. She remembers the day that Kelly had boarded the streetcar to Tacoma, Alex wishing her farewell in tears.

“You were sweethearts.”

She hears Alex sob quietly, and knows it to be true.

“Kelly asked me to be friends, to exchange letters and visit each other. She doesn’t love him like she loved me, she said, but he is kind to her and their house and garden are nice. But I simply can’t, Kara. I told her to leave, to stop breaking my heart anymore.” The words rush out as if they’ve been on the tip of Alex’ tongue for far too long.

“I’m sorry, Alex. I had no idea.”

“We couldn’t risk anyone knowing. I think mother may have had an inkling, but she never asked.” Alex voice is somber, and her sobs have quieted.

“I understand.” Kara says it, and yet she is unsure if she does.

Kelly and Alex had been sweethearts, for how long she doesn’t know. The Danvers and the Olsens had been friends since their school days. Kara had even gone steady with James for a week or so when she was 17, and she suppresses a chuckle remembering their awkwardness.

Once more she is reminded of herself and Lena, and Alex’ vehement disapproval of her acquaintance with the Luthor.

“Do you understand now, Kara?” Alex asks as if reading her thoughts. “Do you understand why I wished to keep you apart from Lena?”

Kara takes in a sharp breath. “How-“

“How do I know you’re sweethearts?” Alex asks, a slight edge to her voice. She lets out an audible breath.

“I’ve seen the way you are with her, plain and simple. How you talk about her, how you look at her. I was reminded of myself and Kelly each and every time.” She almost whispers the last sentence.

“We’re not,” Kara starts. “We’re not sweethearts. We-“

“Kara, do not mistake me for a fool.”

“We’ve exchanged kisses, twice. And I like to hold her hand, but-“ Kara doesn’t how to finish the sentence.

There is truth to what Alex has said. She likes to talk about Lena, she is happy when Lena is around, and she had kissed Lena twice. And yet, she and Lena had never stopped to think what it would mean for them. Lena had never called Kara her sweetheart. Kara had never told Lena she loved her.

Love. Does Kara love Lena? Does Lena love her? Does kissing Lena mean that she is her sweetheart? The quiet darkness of the room suddenly seems to suffocate her, seems to take a hold of her like the smoke from her nightmare. She gasps for air, then stands up from her bed so suddenly it makes her head spin.

“Kara? Are you alright?” Alex’ concerned voice comes through to her as if from far away.

Kara rushes through the room and down the stairs, not caring that the creaking of the door or her footsteps on the stairs might wake Eliza. She opens the backdoor and sits down on a small boulder next to their house in the quiet alley. The half-moon illuminates the puddles that fill up the potholes in the ground.

Her mind is filled with memories of Lena. Lena smiling at her with their feet in the cold stream of the creek, Lena observing her in the quiet of the study at the Luthor mansion, Lena kissing her temple as Kara cries in her arms.

She loves Lena. The realization is as sudden as it is definite, and she cannot fathom how she hadn’t known before. She knows there are women who long for the company of other women, who write poetry about the fairer sex. And yet, to know that it feels like this, to know that she had felt it towards Lena this entire time …

It makes her feel calm, like her feet touching solid ground after a stormy night at sea. And yet, there are new questions in her mind. Does Lena love her, too? Does Lena know that they could be sweethearts?

Before her thoughts can wander towards Lena’s French magazines and her fascination with the Parisian dancer who loved another woman unashamed, she hears footsteps approaching. It’s Alex, and Kara silently scoots over to allow her sister to sit on the edge of the boulder with her.

“I didn’t mean to make you scared or feel ashamed.” Alex says, but Kara shakes her head.

“You did not. In fact, it brought me some clarity.” She says and gives her a small smile.

Alex says nothing, but then she reaches out and squeezes Kara’s arm. “I do hope you hear from her soon. I hope you can have some certainty about your future.”

Kara swallows thickly. “Thank you for trusting me. I’m sorry I never asked you more about Kelly.”

She had been too distracted by Lena, Kara thinks, regret forming in her stomach. Alex had been nursing a broken heart all this time, while Kara had been out and about with Lena. After Jeremiah’s passing, Kara had to once more lament the loss of a parent. Alex had stepped into his role, doing her best to run their business, but they had somehow lost the sisterly bond they had just established.

When Alex doesn’t respond, seemingly lost in her own thoughts, Kara speaks up again. “You’ve lost so much, too. I’m sorry I only ever saw my own grief. When I started being friends with Lena, I was so happy to be with her that I forgot you were still nursing your own wounds. We used to tell each other everything, remember? Laying awake at night, speaking about our dreams for the future.” Kara lets out a small huff.

There is a beat of silence, then Kara goes on. Her voice is softer now. “Jeremiah would be so proud of you, Alex. I know it. He would be so proud of you for keeping his business alive despite the recession, and for not being afraid to love.”

When she turns her head to look at Alex, she can see tears streaming down her face. She shifts to put her arms around her. Alex remains silent, but there is no need for words. For the first time in a long while, Kara feels connected to her adoptive sister, feels the bond they used to share untangle and invigorate.

They sit like this for a while until Alex yawns. “It’s getting cold out here. Let’s go back inside and sleep before mother asks tomorrow why we’re tired.”

Kara chuckles slightly and nods. Sleep finds them both at once, and in the morning, Kara feels like a weight has been lifted off her chest. When she hears Alex laugh in the kitchen with Eliza, she knows the same is true for her sister.

 

***

 

“Hurry, Kara. We mustn’t miss the streetcar!” Eliza shouts from downstairs.

Sitting on the stool in front of her vanity, Kara giggles. “We have more than enough time, Eliza! I need my hair to look decent.”

She cannot hear her adoptive mother’s response, but she knows she is mumbling something under her breath. She hears the door to the kitchen close and imagines her talking to Charlie, fixing Jeremiah’s old bowtie on his collar that they had given him for the day.

Next to her, Alex tries to take a glimpse into the looking glass. “Where do you keep the hairpins, Kara? My hair looks a fright.”

“The hairpins wouldn’t help th- ow!” Kara yelps when Alex hits her shoulder. Giggling, she hands Alex the old sour ball tin box.

It’s a Sunday, and the mood in the Danvers household is as bright as it hasn’t been in a long while. They’re about to head out to the library to attend James’ photography event. Just a little over a week ago, spending 4 cents on a photograph would have seemed outrageous. Today, however, they welcome the day off and the distraction from the hard work of the week.

When they enter the kitchen, Alex whistles at Charlie. “You look quite dapper, buddy!”

The boy grins. The bowtie looks comically big in comparison to his small figure, and the jacket is far from new and reaches until his hands, but he looks proud nonetheless. His long hair is braided tidily. Out of the four of them, he may well be the most excited to visit the library and meet James.
