Kara silently thanks the good heavens for Kelly’s visit the day before. It may have made Alex weep all day into the night, but it evidently lifted a weight off her sister’s chest. Kara’s, too. She is sure that otherwise Alex would have not joined them for the library, having avoided even James since Kelly’s departure.

A sudden, energetic knock on the front door makes them all freeze in their spots. Alex looks at Kara first, then Eliza. “Could it be the police? Should we hide Charlie?” she whispers.

Kara bites her lip to keep herself from letting out a yelp. Working on Sundays had been prohibited for many years, at least since Kara can remember. Most stores in the city, Danvers Footwear included, had worked the previous Sunday in light of the arrival of the steamer Portland and the prospective miners buying gear and outfits.

“The store is closed on Sundays! We are at leisure!” Eliza shouts. Kara waits with bated breath.

There is a moment of silence until a female voice calls out. “It’s Sam and Ruby!”

The four of them let out a collective breath. Alex and Kara rush to the front door, Charlie and Eliza following behind.

“Oh.” Alex says in lieu of greeting when she opens the door.

Sam is wearing a fashionable Sunday dress with a matching hat. Ruby is dressed neatly, as well. Alex stares at Sam for a second, and Kara watches as Sam observes her sister quietly in return. She clears her throat, and it makes Alex jump slightly.

“Oh. Right. What brings you here?” she asks, rubbing her hand on her skirt.

Sam clears her throat as well. “We just came back from Sunday mass.” She turns to Kara. “We saw Lena and her mother.”

Kara’s eyes go wide. “How is she? Did you talk to her?”

“No, I’m afraid there was no chance to speak to her. They left at once when mass was over. I only exchanged a glance with Lena during service, and she gave me a sad smile. Lillian seemed solemn as well, even if I have never seen her joyous to begin with.”

“Oh.” Kara cannot help but feel crestfallen.

“I nevertheless thought it was worthy of sharing with you” Sam adds and gives Kara a small smile.

Kara looks up. “It was. Thank you.”

Before she can ask more questions, Eliza speaks up. “I’m afraid we need to hurry.”

“We were just on our way to the library to get our photographs taken” Alex explains to Sam, who looks flustered.

“I apologize for holding you up. Ruby and I will be on our way then.”

It is in that moment, however, that Ruby notices Charlie standing next to Eliza, half hidden in the entrance of the shop. She breaks into a wide smile. “Mother, may I play baseball with Charlie, please?”

Sam looks between Ruby, Charlie and Alex, seeming unsure of how to respond.

“Why don’t you accompany us to the library? You two are already dressed impeccably, you might get your photograph taken as well.” Kara rushes to ask.

She had promised Charlie to play baseball with Ruby, but she had also promised to take him to the library. It seems like a good opportunity to make good on both of those promises. Besides, with the new knowledge that Alex had been sweethearts with Kelly and is trying to forget her, she hasn’t missed the way her sister had looked at Lena’s friend. She knows Sam to be married, however the woman had seemed intrigued by Alex, and Kara is nothing if not meddlesome.

Sam seems to think about it, but Ruby tugs on her sleeve and asks “Please?”, and Sam finally nods. “Alright. As long as we do not intrude.”

“Not at all” Kara says and smiles, and ignores the way Alex glances at her.

Eliza once again urges them to hurry, and so the party gets on their way. They walk up Harbor Road towards the nearest street car stop, making it there just in time. It is not a long ride towards Pioneer Square, and the street car is empty save for a gentleman reading a newspaper. Charlie and Ruby chatter loudly while Alex and Sam exchange polite conversation.

Eliza reaches out to squeeze Kara’s hand from the seat across from her. “Are you alright? We all worry about Lena.”

Kara only nods and smiles at her adoptive mother. “I hope to find a way to visit her or send her a letter without raising the suspicion of her family. I will talk to Sam about it once more.”

The library is more busy than usual, and with families walking around in their best dress, it almost resembles a Sunday service. Almost all families seem to be working families like themselves. The patterns of the dresses, the cuts of the jackets and the style of the hats paint a colorful picture of the fashion since the beginning of the decade. While photography has become an amateur hobby in recent years, there are still many who cannot spare the dollars for the equipment and the film. It makes Kara all the more grateful for James to hold the event.

They walk into the chamber the event is held in, a bright room with high ceilings. Charlie and Ruby look around in wonder as they wait. When it’s their turn and James spots them, he put his camera down on his stool and gives Kara a warm hug.

“Kara! What a pleasure to see you! How have you been?” His smile is as friendly as ever, and Kara realizes how she had missed him. She had not been to the library in recent weeks.

When he sees Alex, however, a slight frown clouds his expression. “Alex. I have not seen you in ages. I hope you are well?”

Kara glances between her friend and her sister. She wonders if Kelly had told her brother of her visit the previous day. It suddenly hits her that James might have known all this time that Alex and Kelly had been sweethearts. She swallows. 

“Thank you, James. I am well. I hope you are, too.” Alex replies, and James gives her a small smile.

He makes no mention of Kelly, and Kara finds herself eager to change the subject. “We have had much work in the last week, with the miners and all.”

James turns back towards her. “Oh yes, I can imagine. I have been quite busy as well. I have been taking my new camera on my daily walks, taking photographs of the chaos that has befallen the city.” He chuckles. “I wish I had seen the ship dock. It would have made an excellent photograph.”

“I did see it!” Kara says, and at James’ surprised reaction tells him of her morning at the docks a week prior.

“Would you show me your camera?” she asks when she has finished, before James can ask her more about the Luthors. James nods and gestures for all of them to take a closer look at the camera.

Charlie makes wide eyes, tentatively reaching out to touch the case. James seems to see the boy for the first time and he shoots Kara a questioning glance.

“This is Charlie, our apprentice.” She introduces him, and Charlie smiles at James. James smiles back at the boy, and gives him a short explanation of the workings of his camera. Sam and Ruby stand next to them, listening closely as well.

“The camera can fit in my satchel, it’s most convenient” James raves. “It’s affordable, too, and I have joined a Photography Club recently. Anyone can pick up photography as a hobby now; all you need to do is press the shutter!”

Eliza smiles. “Maybe we can buy a Kodak next year if business keeps going well.”

Alex stares at her mother with wide eyes at the statement, but says nothing. Once again Kara is reminded how fast their lives have changed, how many things are on the horizon of possibility now. And they aren’t even the ones to own the gold.

They get their photographs taken. Individual shots, a shot of Alex and Kara, the four of them, and of course Sam and Ruby together. Sam insists on sitting down on the chair and facing the other wall so that she might look at the camera from over her shoulder. James laughs, it is apparently a very fashionable way to get one’s photograph taken these days. Ruby covers her face in embarrassment of her mother’s antics.

Afterwards, Kara and Charlie head towards the book shelves, and Kara borrows ‘The adventures of Tom Sawyer’ for him as she had promised. The clerk at the counter, who is substituting for James, gives Charlie a strange look, but Kara decides to ignore him. Her day has been far too enjoyable to let it get spoiled by a stranger.

When they leave the library, they head to the nearest park. It’s sunny, and neither of them is in the mood to go home yet. Especially not Charlie and Ruby, who start playing tag as soon as their shoes touch the grass. Sam buys them all lemonade from a stand nearby, and they sit down on a bench to chat.

Alex and Sam talk among each other, and Kara wonders what it is they could be conversing about. At one point, Alex throws her head back and laughs heartily, and Kara doesn’t miss the smile that forms on Eliza’s face at the sound. It makes Kara realize how long it has been since she’s heard the sound, and she takes a sip of her lemonade before she can tear up at the thought.

Sam’s recount of seeing Lena at church in the morning is still in the back of her mind, making her heart feel heavy. Nevertheless, she feels a sort of contentment, and it makes her wish for a camera to capture this moment in time.

8. Chapter 8

Notes for the Chapter:

part 2 is here! thank you for reading and i hope you guys like it!

cw for minor character death in this chapter

Part II

- East -

1897

„Would you like another cup of coffee, Miss Luthor?” Eve asks as she enters the dining room where Lena is sitting alone at the table. The rest of her family have finished their breakfast a while ago, Lillian having retreated to the upstairs chamber to sit by Lionel’s bed.

It’s a scene that has repeated itself countless times in the last two weeks in the Luthor household. Lena swallows the rest of her omelet and bread, washing it down with a gulp of water. She shakes her head.

“No, thank you, Eve. Please clean up the table. I will retreat to my chamber now.”

Eve nods and starts placing Lena’s plate and cup onto a tray. She sends Lena a concerned glance, but Lena pretends not to notice. The chair creaks on the floorboard as she gets up. She is making her way up the stairs when she hears voices coming from the study.

She pauses in the upstairs hallway. The door to the study is slightly ajar, and she can see the shadow of her brother pacing back and forth in quick steps. “We would not make it all the way to Dawson, or even the mines. I think Skagway or Whitehorse is an agreeable option. Many miners will be stuck there once the Yukon river freezes over. It will be good for business,” her brother says, and she can hear Benoit mumble something in agreement.

Lena suppresses a groan and walks back towards her own chamber. It is a conversation the men have had many a times this fortnight. Lex is adamant about going back to the Yukon. Not to mine for more gold, but instead to do business with desperate miners. Lena has thought it silly since the moment it had been suggested. However, no one had asked her of her opinion, so she had kept silent.

A half hour later, there is a knock on her door. Lena is lounging on her bed. She has been keeping herself occupied with reading anything she can find, from Jack’s letters, to the magazines, to books she finds in the study. Anything to keep her mind off how much she misses Kara, or how poorly her father’s health is. She tends to visit him in his chamber in the evening, even if their topics of conversation are sparse.

When she opens the door, Eve is standing there. Her face is pale, and she avoids meeting Lena’s eyes. “Your father wishes to speak to you, Miss Luthor.”

Lena’s heartbeat picks up, all the blood in her body rushing to her head, making her ears ring. She nods and follows Eve, but she might as well be sleep-walking down the hallway. When they enter the chamber, Lillian is dabbing at her eyes with her handkerchief, and when she sees Lena come in, she looks as if she’s seeing a ghost.

“Lena, my child. Sit down.” Lionel’s voice is hoarse, and when Lena’s eyes fall on him, she is shocked to see how poorly he looks. His face seems much paler than just the previous night, his hair grayer. He is sitting up, propped up by pillows, but his features cannot conceal the pain he appears to be in.

Lena wordlessly sits down on the stool next to the bed. She looks from Lionel to Lillian.

“Lionel, do you really wish to do this?” Lillian whispers.

Lionel nods. His eyes are tired. “I do, Lillian. I have to. For my own peace.” He clears his throat. “Would you leave us alone?”

Lillian sighs, then gets up and gestures for Eve to follow her. Eve places a hand on Lena’s shoulder when she walks past her, but instead of finding the gesture comforting, it only adds to Lena’s sense of dread.

The room is only dimly lit, and Lena can see heavy, dark clouds through the window from where she is sitting. “What do you have to tell me, father?”

Lionel takes a long moment to look at her. Lena adverts her eyes, finding the sadness in his eyes and the pain evident on his face unbearable to observe.

“You look just like your mother.” Lionel’s voice is barely above a whisper.

Lena feels the words like a punch to the throat. “You knew my mother?” she croaks out. She can feel tears forming at the back of her eyes.

Lionel only nods, then gestures for her to come closer. When she does, he takes her hand in hers. Lena startles at how cold his hands feel. “I did. She was a wonderful woman. Beautiful and kind, and with an enchanting accent. You take after her in so many ways, and of that I’ve always been incredibly proud.” He gives her a sad smile.

Lena can feel her blood rushing in her ears, her world narrowing down to the feeling of her own clammy hand gripping Lionel’s. She is about to ask how he knew her, why he is telling her this, when Lionel continues.

“You are my child, Lena. It was no coincidence that I came to Dublin all those years ago to adopt you. I looked for you when I heard of your mother’s death. The father you thought you never knew, is me.” His hands grip Lena’s even tighter.

Lena takes in a sharp breath. She tries to retract her hands, but her father holds on just tightly enough. The room seems to be spinning around her, and she closes her eyes. Her chin trembles, and when she opens her eyes again her vision is blurry from tears.

“So I am a real Luthor,” she whispers.

“You are. You are my blood, and you and Lex will be the future of our business.”

Lena bites the inside of her cheek to keep herself from sobbing out loud. She thinks about Lex and Benoit in the study, deciding their - and her - future. She thinks about Lillian, somewhere in the house, having tried to keep Lionel from revealing his secret to her.

Lionel goes on. “I was unfaithful to Lillian, after Lex was born. I went to London and Dublin many times; I spent many more months there than in New York. When your mother died and I confessed my affair to Lillian and told her of my wish to adopt you, she was obviously hurt. She dealt with it by keeping away from you, by giving all of her attention to Lex. I swore to her to never talk of your mother again. Forgive me, Lena.”

Lena sits still, unable to move or speak. Forgiveness has never come easy to her. She thinks of the many hours she used to spend wondering why the Luthors had chosen her, why her fate kept being decided by things out of her control. At last, the answers to those questions are bared, right in front of her. It does not make the ache in her chest disappear, however.

“I need to think about this alone” she says after a few moments.

Lionel sighs, but lets go of her hand. He gives her another long look. “Lena, I put all my hope in you and Lex for the future of our family business. You will marry Benoit and attend a good college, and become a good wife and mother. I am at peace now that I have told you what I could not for so many years.”

Lena stares at her father, tries to comprehend the meaning behind his words. It feels as if he is biding farewell to her. He looks exhausted, even more so than when Lena had entered the chamber. Suddenly, she cannot stand to be around him any longer. She gets up abruptly, curtsies, and retreats to her own chamber.

The tears start falling when she flings herself onto her bed. Her sobs are loud and irregular, but she doesn’t care if anyone can hear. They can listen to her cry for all she cares. Not only Lionel – her father – had lied to her for years. Lillian had too, and Lex as well. They had lied to her, forbidden her to speak of Ireland and her mother, and for what reason? So that her father might not have to confront his past mistakes? So that Lillian might pretend she has a happy marriage? Lena buries her face in a pillow to absorb her scream.

She loses track of time. Her sobs recede eventually, and she lies there motionlessly. Heavy drops of rain start to fall, and she listens as they hit her window in an irregular rhythm. Noon is still hours away, yet the day outside is gray in gray.

“Miss Luthor.” Lena startles when Eve’s voice comes from the door. She turns around and sits up. She had not heard her knock or walk in.

Eve gives Lena a look of sympathy she has never observed on her maid before. It is only after a moment that she sees the white sheet Eve is holding in her hands. Lena’s eyes go wide.

“I’m sorry, Miss Luthor.” Eve says and walks towards Lena’s vanity, covering the looking glass.

Lena stares at her maid. Only then she becomes aware of the commotion in the house. Fast, heavy footsteps can be heard from the stairs, and then Lillian’s cries and Lex’ voice, comforting his mother.

Despite having just learned that she shares flesh and blood with the Luthors, Lena has never felt more disconnected from her family than in this moment. She feels like a stranger in her own chamber, merely watching as strangers grieve the loss of someone she has barely known. Eve seems to want to say something, but Lena doesn’t wait for her to do so.

She jumps from the bed and reaches for the first pair of shoes she can find. They’re her Sunday shoes, the ones Lionel bought her before their departure to the Yukon. The shoes which clasp she tore off to have a reason to visit the Danvers shop. It feels like a lifetime ago, suddenly.

Eve says something to her, but Lena doesn’t hear her. Her chamber, the house, Eve, Lex, Lillian, Benoit, it all feels far away. She runs out of her chamber and down the stairs, nearly colliding with a maid. She grabs the first hat she sees and runs out of the front door.

Outside, the rain is pouring, the sidewalk slippery, the road muddy. Lena is soaked in seconds, her blouse clinging to her body, her socks stained beyond mending. Yet, she runs and runs until the harbor comes into sight. Sailors are huddled under the roof of one of the oyster bars, smoking, trying to stay warm and dry. The clouds hang low on the water, and the docking ships are barely visible. She turns onto Harbor Road, counting the number of run-down storefronts and illuminated windows until she reaches a familiar door.

She opens it with unsteady hands, and when she steps into the shop, she barely registers the people staring at her until her eyes find Kara. Kara is standing there, her eyes wide in surprise, her hair glowing butter yellow in front of the lamp.

Lena rushes towards her, and once she’s in the safety of her embrace, she faints in Kara’s arms.

 

***

 

Kara sits on her stool in the shop, working on a pair of rubber boots with Charlie. They have just opened for the day, but two clients are already at the counter, placing their orders with Alex. Outside, heavy rain is falling.

When the front door gets pushed wide open all of a sudden and a figure stumbles into the shop, Kara jumps up in surprise. She’s ready to fight the intruder and shove them outside when she freezes in her spot. For a moment, her world stops turning as she recognizes who it is. Standing in the entrance of the shop, soaked by the rain and out of breath, is Lena. Her hair and her clothes are clinging to her body, her eyes darting around the room until they land on Kara.

Then, Lena sets in motion again and rushes towards her. All Kara can do is open her arms on instinct to catch Lena as she all but falls into her embrace. She wants to say something, to ask Lena what has happened, when she feels Lena’s body become heavy. When she looks down, she sees with alarm that Lena’s eyes are closed, and she can feel Lena’s hands fail to grip her shirt.

“Kara! What’s wrong?” Alex is by her side in a heartbeat, helping her lift Lena so she doesn’t sink to the ground.

“I don’t know.” Kara whispers. They manage to sit Lena down on Kara’s stool, her back leaning heavily against the work table. “Lena …”

The clients are staring at them, and Kara feels a panic rising in her chest that they might recognize Lena, that it might start gossip. She moves to shield Lena from their view.

“I’ve got this” she whispers to Alex, who regards Lena with a worried look, then nods.

Kara gently caresses Lena’s cheek, holding her in place with her other hand. Slowly, Lena’s eyes open. She blinks, then her gaze focuses on Kara. She smiles. “Kara …”

“Lena.” Kara swallows thickly. Lena’s face looks even paler than usual in the dim light of the shop. “I’m here. You’re alright.”

Charlie hands Kara a clean towel that he must have gotten from the kitchen. Kara thanks him and smiles at the thoughtfulness of the boy, then dabs at Lena’s forehead. Her hair is dripping, and her lips quiver.

“You’re going to catch a cold,” Kara whispers. “Can you climb the stairs? You should lie down.” She takes both of Lena’s hands into her own.

Lena nods hesitantly. With Charlie’s help, Kara walks Lena out of the shop and away from the curious eyes of the men. They move upstairs into Alex’ and Kara’s chamber.

It is only then that Kara realizes she cannot let Lena climb into her bed with her soaked clothes. She instructs Charlie to go downstairs again to help Alex, then gets a clean night gown from her drawer.

“Put this on” she tells Lena. “I’ll go make tea. Is that alright?”

Lena still hasn’t spoken, but she nods and grips the fabric tightly. As Kara waits for the water to boil in the kitchen, her imagination runs wild with the things Lena could have been running away from. Had it been something her family had done or said? Or her fiancé? Or had she been walking by herself and gotten surprised by the rain? She quickly pours the tea, then goes back upstairs.

Lena is sitting on her bed in the night gown, drying her hair with the towel. Kara has never seen it like this before, without the hairpins and the tight bun. She stops short on the threshold, her stomach doing somersaults.

“Thank you, Kara.” Lena whispers, her voice hoarse as she smiles up at Kara.

Kara hands her the cup, then sits down next to her. “Pray tell, what’s the matter?”

Lena’s face turns solemn. It takes her a moment to respond. “My father passed away this morning.”

“I’m sorry, Lena.” Kara reaches for Lena’s free hand and squeezes it.

She looks at Lena then, really takes the time to look at her. It’s the first time in two weeks that she’s with her, yet it feels like a lifetime since she and Lena had been sitting in the kitchen eating pastries. Lena is as beautiful as Kara remembers her, but there is a tiredness in her features that Kara wishes she could erase. Lena takes a sip of the tea, then sighs.

“Lionel was in poor health when they came back from the Klondike. I haven’t been allowed to leave the house except for church with Lillian,” Lena recalls quietly.  “I’m sure you’ve read about the gold in the papers?”

“I have” Kara whispers. “I saw the ship dock that morning. I saw Lex, Lionel and Benoit with their sacks of gold. We’ve been busy outfitting the prospective miners ever since. The boy earlier, that’s Charlie, our apprentice.” She bites her lip. “So much has changed.”

Lena turns and looks at her with surprise etched into her tired features. Their faces are close, and Kara wishes she could close the small distance between them, to capture Lena’s lips with her own now that she is finally by her side once more.

“Lionel told me a family secret before he passed.” Lena swallows thickly. She turns away again, staring at the wall as if she can see beyond it and into the chambers of her own house.

She remains silent for a while, but Kara waits patiently for her to continue until she notices the tea cup shaking in Lena’s hand. She takes it from her and places it onto the floor, then moves closer towards her. She wraps her arm around Lena’s torso, resting her head on her shoulder the same way she has done dozens of times. Lena hums, placing her own hand above Kara’s to keep her there.

“He is my real father. I’m a real Luthor, after all.” Lena says it so quietly that Kara almost misses it, caught up in the sensation of being close to her again, of seeing her in her own night gown, of droplets of water tickling her cheek.

She takes in a deep breath. Not knowing what to say, if Lena wishes for her to speak at all, she only pulls Lena closer. Lena leans into her touch, a sad smile tugging at her lips. Kara thinks that she never wants to let Lena go again, that she would like to keep her with her forever instead of with a family that withheld the truth from her for so many years.

They fall back into the pillows when Kara shifts, and a small chuckle escapes Lena’s lips. The sound is divine to Kara’s ears, and she pulls Lena into her arms properly. Their fronts are pressed together, and she can feel Lena shiver. She rubs soothing circles onto her back with her thumb, hoping to provide warmth and comfort.

“You’re so much more than your family, Lena, whether they are your blood or not. You’re smart and kind, and most dear to me.”

Lena looks up, her face no more than an inch away from Kara’s. Kara can see the freckle on her neck, and a memory of her putting her lips on Lena’s skin there surfaces in her mind. She doesn’t realize she’s staring until she can feel the press of Lena’s lips against her own. At first, she thinks the sensation to be in her memory, but then Lena whimpers and Kara is brought back to the present in an instant.

Despite their mutual insistence, half a lifetime ago, that it would be ‘just this once’, it’s neither an accident nor a mistake, and Kara can feel heat spreading through her entire body. She kisses Lena back, her hand moving towards the back of her neck to keep her close.

She becomes acutely aware of Lena’s chest pressing against her own through the thin fabric of her night gown, she herself having forgone wearing a corset in favor of her work attire. Lena shifts ever so slightly, her hand wandering up Kara’s side slowly in exploration.

“I missed you so much,” Lena mumbles against her lips, and Kara lets out a shaky breath.

“Me too.” She searches Lena’s face. “I never want to be separated from you again.”

Lena rushes forward to reconnect their lips, stealing the air from Kara’s lungs once more. The kiss is more fervent than the first, and when Kara touches Lena’s cheek she notices she’s crying.

“They want to go North again,” Lena rushes out before Kara can inquire, “Lex and Benoit. The gold was not enough for them, they want more, to make business where the miners will be. I don’t know what will become of these plans now, but they’ve been adamant about leaving before the winter.”

Kara is stunned, unable to respond. They had enjoyed such great freedoms in their absence, and now the men seem to wish to take Lena away from her for good. She thinks it unfair. They may be Lena’s half-brother and fiancé, but Kara is the one who Lena runs to, who she tells her secrets to, who she kisses and comforts. Kara closes her eyes.

“They cannot take you from me, Lena. I shall not know how to go on.”

Lena hums. “Me neither. However, I fear that it is yet another instance of something beyond my control, beyond our control. This city has become my home, if I ever knew what such a thing was. Yet now … we’ve spoken of this before, do you recall? The world is unjust, and these things are beyond our control.”

“Let us not speak of this now,” Kara whispers and swallows thickly. She can feel tears prickling behind her eyelids, and she finds the moment most inept to cry when she is the one supposed to comfort Lena.

Lena gives her another solemn smile, then kisses Kara again. It wipes Kara’s mind blank of any worrisome thoughts, and she lets herself get lost in the sensation of feeling Lena so close to her once more. After a while, Lena tugs herself into Kara’s embrace, and they simply lie there in the company of each other.

They sit up when they hear footsteps on the stairs. The rain has stopped, Kara notices, and she wonders how much time has passed.

“It’s noon, Alex and I have closed the shop for our break.” It’s Charlie, and he stands in the doorframe looking at his feet.

“There is a woman at the door, Miss Lena. She wants to take you home.”

Kara feels her heart stop beating, and she and Lena look at each other in alarm. No one in the Luthor household knows of their friendship, as far as they are aware.

“Could it possibly be my mother?” Lena asks, her voice hoarse.

Kara notices how Lena’s face is flush and her lips are swollen, and she blushes deeply.

“No, Miss Lena. She said her name was Eve.” Charlie is still not looking up at either of them.

Eve is Lena’s maid, Kara remembers, and judging from the look of both relief and surprise on Lena’s face, this is only a slightly better option than Lillian.

“I will come downstairs shortly,” Lena states and gets up, collecting her wet clothing from the chair in front of the vanity. Charlie nods and returns downstairs.

Lena takes off the night gown as if she has forgotten Kara’s presence and shivers in the cool air of the room. Kara’s blush deepens and she turns her gaze away.

“I do wonder how Eve knows I came here,” Lena mumbles.

Once Lena leaves, Kara is left to ponder the same question, as well as wonder when she will see Lena again, or at all.

 

***

 

Lena stares at her reflection in the looking glass as if she’s seeing her own face for the first time. She’s sitting at her vanity, Eve making a fuss about her hair behind her, tucking in hair pins and rearranging the black satin hairband Lillian had instructed Lena wear as part of her mourning attire.

“Eve, I do not care much for my hair today. Let me put on a hat and bring this day to an end so I may go back to sleep.” Lena yawns as she says it, and she resists the urge to wipe at her eyes.

Eve sighs. “Miss Luthor, I understand you are tired, but you must look agreeable for church. You may go back to sleep at once when you return, and I will have a cup of tea ready for you.”

Lena says nothing, only picks at her sleeve. She dislikes the stiffness of the material, its chemical smell and dreadful color. After Eve had taken her home from the Danvers shop the day before, Lena had spent the rest of the day in bed. She had run a low fever that has passed since, but she had caught a cold as Kara had feared, her throat sore and her head aching.

Lillian had shown little sympathy, too in distress over Lionel’s passing and the arrangements that needed to be made. Lex and Benoit had gone downtown to arrange the obituary to be put into the papers and to send a telegram to their office in New York.

Upon their arrival at the house, Eve had exclaimed in a dramatic fashion: “Mrs. Luthor, I found your poor daughter wandering by the harbor in the rain all by herself! She was most distraught, but I was able to convince her to come back home!”

Lena had been too stunned to question Eve’s reasoning for her lie about her whereabouts, and too dazed in the onset of her fever to care much for it. Lillian had simply dismissed them to Lena’s chamber, instructing she take a bath and be presentable for church by the morning.

After Eve is done fussing about her hair, Lena applies her favorite perfume and gives Eve a long, questioning look through the looking glass. Eve looks away and towards her feet, but Lena does not miss the blush creeping up her maid’s neck. Eve seems to have an inkling about the question Lena is about to ask.

“Pray tell, why did you not tell Lillian where I really was yesterday, Eve?” Lena asks and clears her throat. “Or I should ask, how did you know where I was at all?”

Eve slowly looks up, and Lena can see her swallowing. “Us servant folk are more observant than you might guess, Miss Luthor.” She bites her lip. “I have been aware of your friendship with the shoemaker girl. Your generous nature has been something I’ve admired since the day I started working for you. Not every lady will ask her maid to sit down with her for a cup of tea.”

At this, Lena cannot help but blush herself. To her, these are acts of simple kindness. If she has to be in Eve’s presence for hours each day, she thinks it only natural to want to be friendly with her. Especially since she still finds herself missing Jess, the maid who had worked for her since Lena had been in her teens.

“What has kept you from telling of it to my mother? You must know she would be most angry with me, but laud you for your honesty and loyalty towards her.” Lena does not intent her tone to be accusing, yet she cannot help the edge to her voice. She feels tired, and almost regrets starting this topic of conversation at all.

“I simply did not wish to upset you or cause you pain by keeping you from enjoying the company of your friend, simply because her status does not allow for you to be publicly acquainted.”

Eve says it so matter-of-factly, yet somehow it does not help to quiet the nerves Lena feels talking about Kara. She has no way of knowing if Eve knows more than she lets on, knows what Lena and Kara have done when no one is watching them, what they had been doing the previous day until Eve had shown up.

“Besides, once we all move North, you will be separated from your dear friend in any way, so it is of no use to make a fuss about it now.” Eve adds, and Lena feels the words like a dagger in her chest.

Then, however, she pauses. “We? Have they decided to take you along? I hear there are plenty of desperate men and women in the North, seeking employment. Why would you leave behind the civility of the city when you can be hired by another family here?”

“Oh no, we haven’t talked of it yet.” Eve says, avoiding Lena’s gaze again. “But your brother has just come back, and I would be happy to accompany your family, Miss Luthor. Your brother is a most kind and humble man.” Her cheeks glow red, and Lena turns around in her chair to stare at her maid with an open mouth.

“Lex is kind to the people who can benefit him,” she laughs, the words leaving her mouth before she can stop them. She bites her tongue. Eve covers her face with her hands.

She thinks to tell Eve about the way he talks of the working people, about how back in New York he once laughed into the face of a warehouse worker who asked for a higher wage. About how her brother has been obsessed with the idea of owning gold for years, and now that he has it, he is far from satisfied. Instead, she quietly observes Eve for a moment.

“Do you fancy him, Eve? Is this what makes you loyal to this household?” She almost whispers it, even though she feels like screaming in frustration. Her head is pounding, and the revelation that her maid fancies her brother is something that could have waited another day or two. Or perhaps she wishes to never have come to such a conclusion at all.

Instead of an answer, Eve lets out something akin to a whimper, then turns around and fetches Lena’s Sunday shoes. Lena takes it as a confirmation. She suppresses the urge to laugh at the sheer ridiculousness of it. It would be a most humble act for Lex to know Eve’s name at all.

When Eve helps Lena to put on her shoes, she asks in a timid voice: “Will you tell your brother of my silly infatuation, Miss Luthor?”

Lena shakes her head. “No, Eve. I do not intent to do so. Let’s just call it even and not speak of it again.”

Eve only nods in response. They head down the stairs in silence, and Eve excuses herself as soon as Lillian steps into the hallway. Lillian is dressed in mourning attire as well, and a veil covers her face. She does not greet Lena nor ask about her well-being, and Lena does not attempt a conversation, either.

The Sunday service seems duller to Lena than usual, the music not filling her with joy as it usually does. Their dress draws stares and whispers, and Lena finds herself wishing they had not attended at all. The obituary will not be published in the papers until the next day, and she can practically hear the rumors about Lionel Luthor’s death spreading through the streets of Seattle already. She has not seen Sam and Ruby yet, but she does not dare to turn her head towards their usual seats.

During prayer, Lillian grabs Lena’s hand and holds it tightly. Flabbergasted, Lena stutters her way through the rest of the pater noster. It makes her wonder how Lillian sees her now that she knows she is a real Luthor. Or if, perhaps, the death of her father means that Lillian will be kinder with her, to show compassion for the child she never wanted to be a part of her family.

Lillian, however, makes no mention of it, and once mass is over, she seems to wish to go home as quickly as possible.

“Oh, Mrs. Luthor!” A familiar, agitated voice reaches them as they leave the church. Mrs. Smith, their neighbor, makes her way through the small crowd towards them, her teenage-aged son in tow.

Lillian mumbles something under her breath that Lena cannot make out but is sure is entirely unfit to be uttered on a Sunday in front of a church, and before she knows it, they are being overwhelmed by condolences and well-wishes, Mrs. Smith’s words ringing loudly in her ears.

It offers her an opportunity to go wandering in search of Sam and Ruby. She doesn’t have to search for long until a whirlwind of brown hair and dark green dress comes rushing towards her, tackling in a hug so fierce she has to take a few steps back.

“Ruby!” she exclaims and hugs the girl back just as tightly. “I haven’t seen you in so long!”

“Lena,” Ruby mumbles into the starched fabric of Lena’s jacket. “Why are you in mourning?”

Before Lena can answer, she hears Sam softly calling her name and looks up to find her friend with tears in her eyes. “Lionel …?” she asks, and Lena nods.

Sam gives her a hug as best as she can with Ruby still clinging towards her. “I’m sorry,” she whispers. “I had heard from Kara that he had looked poorly upon their return.”

Lena’s heart skips a beat at Kara’s name. In the same instant she also realizes she has to now tell Sam of her father’s secret, too. She glances towards Lillian, who appears to still be in conversation with their neighbors.

Sam tells her of going to the Danvers shop out of worry for Lena, of talking to Kara and Alex and Ruby making friends with Charlie. Ruby grins at that.

Lena wants to mention that she visited Kara the day before, but something holds her back. She trusts Sam, but she is acutely aware of the possibility of Lillian overhearing her conversation. Besides, she needs Sam to know about her father more urgently. She quickly recaps her last moments with Lionel. Sam’s expression goes from soft and sympathetic to exasperated.

“How very dare they lie to you for all your life, Lena! I cannot believe it!” Sam’s voice grows dangerously loud, and Lena hushes her.

“Lillian mustn’t overhear me telling you this! I am to be in the house at all times for the next two weeks, the funeral will be held within the next week.”

Sam bites her lip. “And then? What will become of the company? Will you marry Benoit once the mourning period has passed?”

Lena looks down and fiddles with the hem of her jacket. Next to her, Ruby is anxiously shifting her weight. “They plan to head North again.” She tells Sam of her brother’s plans.

Sam is in the middle of telling her how outrageous she finds the decision when Lillian comes up to them. She spares no look for Sam and Ruby and simply tugs on Lena’s sleeve, signaling for her to come with her.

“Goodbye, have a good Sunday” Lena manages to get out before they are walking towards their waiting carriage. Sam and Ruby wave after her.

Lena wishes it didn’t feel like saying goodbye to her friend, but she also cannot seem to conjure up a scenario in which they would be carefree again, drinking tea in a fancy parlor downtown or taking a walk by the harbor. She spends the ride in the carriage with her eyes closed the whole way back to the house. All she can see ahead of her are days as dull as a voyage at sea.

9. Chapter 9

Kara hurries along the empty, dimly lit sidewalk, barely daring to glance left or right, especially not towards the mansion on the other side of the street. She clutches the envelope in her hand tightly, the paper crumbled from her grip. At the next intersection, she does stop, and before she crosses the street she turns around.

The Luthor house sits quiet on its lot, dark save for one room downstairs. Kara thinks it might be the sitting room, from the one time she had been inside. A shadow, the silhouette of a female figure passes behind an illuminated curtain, and Kara shudders. It might have been Eve, or another maid, likely not Lena, but she quickly averts her gaze and hurries further along.

The envelope she carries contains a letter for Lena, but she is not headed for her house. Instead, she is going to drop it off at Sam’s, who in turn is going to visit Lena the next day. Kara longs to see Lena, longs to say the words she’s written to her instead of carrying them through the night. She can feel tears burning behind her eyelids that threaten to blur the light of the streetlamps, and she moves even faster so they don’t spill over.

On Sunday, Kara had been playing dominoes with Charlie in the kitchen while Alex and Eliza had been washing the dishes from their lunch. Charlie had moved in with them that morning, Alex helping him carry the little possessions he had from the boarding house. They had put a camp bed into Eliza’s room until they could afford something better.

Eliza had feared the boy would be malcontent or anxious about his new environment, but he had sat at the breakfast table on Monday as if he had always lived there. His shyness from the first days of his working with them had long been gone. Instead, he had held out a comic strip from the newspaper, asking Alex to read it to him. Kara smiles as she turns another street corner. A woman is closing up a flower shop, and they exchange a quiet greeting in passing.

Then, Sam and Ruby had knocked on their door once again. They’d seen Lena at church, Sam said. Ruby stared at Kara with wide eyes as her mother spoke.

“Lionel passed away, and Lex wants to go North again,” Sam whispered.

Kara could feel her cheeks grow hot. She looked down at her feet, then gestured for the two to come inside. “I am already aware” she said. “Lena came here yesterday.”

Sam looked at Kara, and even in the dim light of the shop Kara could see the surprise on her face. “She did not mention that. Then again, she was hurried to go home by her mother. Hmm.”

They entered the kitchen, and Charlie shrieked with joy at seeing Ruby. He immediately demanded Ruby play a game of dominoes with him, and Ruby sat down on Kara’s chair. Kara wanted to say that she and Charlie had to finish their game first, but she found she couldn’t be mad at the boy. She shook her head in amusement and exchanged a smile with Sam.

“Sam, hello! How nice to see you again!” Alex’ voice interrupted, and Kara saw her sister standing behind Sam, cheeks flush and her hands wrung in their dish towel. Eliza was busy rescuing a glass of water from the table before Ruby or Charlie knocked it over.

Sam turned around, and she held eye contact with Alex for a long moment before saying “it’s nice to see you again, too. Are you enjoying your Sunday?”

“We are.” Alex gave her a nervous smile. “Charlie moved in this morning, his lease was up at the boarding house.”

Sam tilted her head, and they both glanced at the table where a game of dominoes was being played whose rules were not to be understood by the grown-ups.

“Would you like water or tea, Mrs. Arias?” Eliza asked politely into the lull of conversation. “I’m afraid we don’t have much space in our house, but we’d be happy to spend a little while in your company.”

Kara noticed Alex rub her neck in a nervous manner, something she did not observe her sister doing very often. She thought it interesting, the effect Sam’s presence seemed to have on her. As much as she wanted to talk to Sam alone, to ask her every little detail about the way Lena had looked this morning at church, she could sense that she didn’t seem to be the only reason Sam and Ruby kept visiting.

Sam seemed to be surprised by something Eliza said for a split second, but she quickly schooled her expression back into a friendly one. “Oh. Tea would be lovely, thank you Mrs. Danvers!”

“Oh please, call me Eliza!” Eliza smiled.

“Alright, then please call me Sam.”

Kara started helping Eliza prepare the tea, and took out cups from the shelf. She decided to offer Sam one of the fancy ones that had once been sold at her parents’ shop, the ones that were so shiny and colorful they seemed out of place in the Danvers’ kitchen.

When she had been younger, she had refused to use them, too afraid to break one and lose another piece of memory of her parents. As the years passed, however, she learned to appreciate seeing them be used instead of collecting dust on the shelves. Before Jeremiah had passed away, they had had regular visitors, friends and colleagues from his work at the City Hall. These days, she tended to use them for Lena’s visits.

The reminder of her friend’s absence made her chest ache, and Kara looked around the room trying to imagine what it would be like if Lena was sitting next to Ruby, laughing along in the careless way Kara didn’t get to hear quite often enough. It would be perfect, she thought.

Eliza’s gentle nudge on her shoulder brought her back to the present, and she put down the cup she had been holding onto the counter.

Once they all sat around the kitchen table, Ruby and Charlie squeezed next to each other sitting on the stools from the shop, Sam spoke up. “Lena came here yesterday?”, she picked up Kara’s earlier statement.

Kara felt her cheeks grow warm as all eyes were on her. “She did. She … she walked through the rain. I …” she swallowed thickly. She had no idea how to express what seeing Lena had meant for her.

She wondered if Lena had told Sam of the secret Lionel had told to his daughter before his passing, or if there had been no time after church for it, either. She imagined Lena in her mourning attire, and hoped she hadn’t caught a cold from the day before.

She shuddered as if she was the one running through a thunderstorm. She gripped the cup of tea in her hands a little more tightly.

Sam hummed. “I thought, maybe I could be of help to you and Lena. It breaks my heart to see the two of you. Lena is not to leave her house during the mourning period, and it would be unwise for you to visit her.”

Kara looked up, and saw that Alex was regarding Sam with interest as well. Eliza nodded in response. Ruby and Charlie had continued their game of dominoes, if quieter now than before. The clack-clack of the tiles on the worn wood of the table cut through the temporary lull in conversation.

“I know,” Kara sighed. “We’ve had an agreement ever since we met that I’m not to go by her house, instead she comes here. I …” she paused.

She remembered the afternoon that she did go to the Luthor house, the afternoon she looked at the books in the expansive study, and then kissed Lena in her chamber. She looked down at the table, hoping no one might guess her thoughts.

“Why don’t you write her a letter?” Sam suggested. “You can bring it to my house, and I will deliver it to Lena. Even if I get turned away at the door, I can still hand her the letter. Lillian has seen me and Ruby at church plenty of times, even if I never had the pleasure of making conversation with her.”

Kara didn’t need to think long to know there was not much else she could do. She nodded, and promised Sam to go by her house within the next days. The conversation moved on then, Eliza asking Sam about her work as a seamstress, and if she could mend an old dress of hers once the chaos in the city had quieted down.

After a while, Ruby asked if she and Charlie could go play baseball on the field again, and Sam shyly looked to Alex for confirmation. It was strange to Kara, since Alex was not Charlie’s parent, and this was something they had seemed to forget to think about. Charlie was still a young boy, not yet a young man, and now that he lived with them, they had some responsibility for him. She wondered if Alex had the same worries, but her sister only smiled and nodded in confirmation.

“Are you coming with us, Kara?” Alex asked when they stood up and Charlie had run upstairs to fetch the glove and baseball.

Kara bit her lip. “No, I wish to be in my room for a while. I will think about what to write to Lena.”

Sam gave her a soft, understanding look and squeezed her shoulder. Kara helped Eliza clean up the cups and the domino tiles once the other had left, and then she went upstairs.

She pulled out her favorite poem collection from her shelf and sat down on the bed. She turned page after page, getting lost in the verses. At each mention of a loving touch, or a kiss on soft lips, she saw Lena in her mind. One poem she read over and over, the words feeling as if she had written them herself, as if this poet from centuries ago had felt the same agony and longing that she was feeling right now.

Kara got up and looked through the drawer in her vanity for a pencil and paper, then sat back on her bed, using the book as an underlay.

 

Dear Lena,

Love shook my heart,

Like the wind on the mountain

Rushing over the oak-trees.

 

I must have read these verses a hundred times before I looked up from the pages of my book and connected the words to you in my heart. I remember the last time we sat together under the oak tree upon the hill. My tears were staining your blouse, but you held me tightly. You kissed my lips after I revealed to you all of my secrets. I miss you dearly, every day that we must spend apart. I long to hold you in my arms, to be the one to listen to all of your secrets and to kiss your lips again. I pray that day comes soon, for I am miserable without you.

 - K

 

Kara finds herself standing in front of Sam’s house, and she takes a deep breath before she knocks, ready to have her most fervent emotions be delivered to Lena through the messy scribble of her handwriting.

 

***

 

Lena sits in the armchair in front of the cold fireplace, the one her father used to sit in their first winter on the West Coast. They hadn’t taken many furniture with them from New York, and this had been one of the new purchases. Her fingers tightly grip the mahogany as she listens to her mother, brother and fiancé argue.

“Lex, you cannot be in your right mind.” Lillian’s voice is agitated, and she’s pacing around the sitting room. “Your disregard for your father’s legacy is quite disagreeable! I wish to stay here until the mourning period has ended properly, and then we might make plans for the future.”

Lex runs a hand over his bald head. “It is too late, mother. I have already purchased the tickets. If we do not leave at once, we will miss valuable business opportunities in the North. Miners are gathering in the towns, and I intend to use the winter for our benefit. I am a businessman first and foremost, just as father was.”

Lena’s eyes go wide, but she knows better than to interrupt their argument. Lex had purchased the tickets for the ship? He surely would not have done so without consulting their mother. Lillian, however, looks just as surprised as Lena. Benoit sits in a chair at the other side of the room, but his face reveals nothing. Lena silently wonders if Lex had been consulting his opinion. She is almost sure of it.

“When did you make the purchase?” Lillian asks. Lena can see her chin trembling. Lillian rarely becomes emotional, but ever since Lionel’s passing, it seems to have become a frequent occurrence.

At the funeral two days before, Lillian had clutched Lena’s hand tightly in the same manner as during church a week ago. Lena had said nothing, not knowing what words would make the woman who had never shown her much sympathy feel better, so she had simply stood there, letting it happen. The funeral passed in a blur; important and fashionably-dressed men that Lena had never seen before coming up to them to express their sympathies. She didn’t cry, at least not in front of the people. Only back in her chamber had she shed a silent tear. However, she was still unsure if she could forgive the Luthors – her family – for not telling her the truth all these years.

“This morning.” Lex’ stern voice cuts through Lena’s thoughts. 

“I intended to announce it at dinner. The ship leaves at 8am on the 14th of this month. It will be the last one before the winter.” Lex says it calmly, but Lena feels as if the room starts to spin. She cannot believe what she is hearing.

Lillian is equally astonished. “You did not think to consult me, Alexander?”

“Mother, I-“ Lex seems taken aback at hearing his full name.

“Lionel would not approve of this. The reasonable thing to do is to stay in Seattle for the winter and wait until the mourning period has passed. You have correspondence to catch up with, correspondence I worked on in your absence. We already have gold in abundance, there is no need to go up North again so soon.” Her voice sounds shaky, if only because Lena is not used to seeing her emotional at all. But she speaks what Lena has been thinking for the past weeks, and Lena waits with bated breath for her brother’s response.

“While I disagree, mother, you may stay here in Seattle and join us in the spring, if you please. I have no plans to sell the house again so soon, as I think a property in a growing city like Seattle is a good investment.” Lex lifts his chin, and it is in this moment that Lena realizes her mother has no chance of convincing him to stay. Their father is dead, and Lex will proceed to behave however he pleases in the name of conducting business for the family. No matter what the ‘family’ thinks.

Lillian crosses her arms. “What about Lena?” she asks after a moment of tense silence.

Lena startles slightly at her presence in the room being addressed to suddenly. She swallows thickly as all eyes in the room are on her.

“She is coming with us, there is no other possibility” Benoit says, and Lillian turns around to him as if she had forgotten he was there at all.

He continues. “We will get married as soon as the mourning period ends. I believe that is in the best interest of everyone involved.”

Lena snorts, then quickly covers her mouth with her hand when everyone turns back to look at her. How did any of them know what her best interest was? She had wanted to marry Jack, someone she actually enjoyed the company of, instead of Benoit who didn’t even address her directly in her father’s presence. Who had never once asked for her opinion or for her well-being.

Lillian seems taken aback by the statement as well. “Have you discussed this?” Lena’s eyes go wide. Since when did Lillian care about her well-being? Her, the very reminder of Lionel’s infidelity, the step-child she had spent years criticizing at every turn for not meeting her expectations of a real Luthor?

“There is nothing to discuss, mother.” Lex says. “Lena will of course come with us. There are not many white women in the North, and she will be valuable company for our colleagues. Whereas here in Seattle, she would be confined to her chamber during the mourning period, and I’m sure she would much prefer the adventure of the unexplored nature. Right, Lena?”

Lena gulps. She wants to scream, wants to tell them how much she hates either option. She hates the idea of walking the dirty roads of an Alaskan town, being gawked at by drunk miners and being cold. She also hates the idea of spending any longer in her stiff mourning attire, unable to leave the house or see Kara. Somehow, however, she cannot bring herself to utter a single syllable. Lex takes it as confirmation, though Lillian looks at her with furrowed brows.

“See, mother? So that is out of the way. Maybe it is even preferable that you stay here, so you can keep up the correspondence with New York. We will be taken several boxes of perfume bottles with us to sell, they will be an inexplicable luxury for the wives of the miners.”

Their argument is over, and Lex talks as if they had reached the conclusion in uniform agreement. Lena stares at her brother, watches the crinkles by his eyes move as he talks. She had been against them looking for gold the first time they went, but he had been excited, so she tried to share his enthusiasm. Now, however, it seems as if his obsession with gold has gotten out of hand.

The Luthors had already been rich, and they will continue to be rich whether they possessed one sack of gold, fifty or none. Lena can somewhat understand miners who leave their families behind with the dream of staking a big claim and making life easier for themselves.

Lex, however, has no such noble aspirations. He wants the gold for the gold’s sake. It had cost their father’s health and then life, and he will leave his mother behind, and he will force his sister into a marriage and a life she does not want.

Suddenly, it’s all too clear to Lena that her brother cannot be reasoned with. He hasn’t taken a single look into the company’s books since he came back, not read a single letter or telegram her mother had exchanged with New York. Lex will sacrifice not only his family, but also his family’s company in the long run, Lena realizes.

Benoit gets up from his chair and runs a hand through his hair. “Well, I will head back to my hotel, then. I will stop by again tomorrow to discuss further matters with you, Mrs. Luthor. We need to decide if we take any of your maids with us or if we have to hire a new one on such short notice. Or maybe we can hire a native when we get up North, I hear they work well and don’t demand as much pay.”

Lena wants to scream once more, wants to tell Benoit that he is not part of their family just yet, that he does not get to make decisions that neither she or Lillian get any say in. But she remains silent, digging her fingernails into the expensive mahogany.

Benoit excuses himself, and shortly after Lillian announces she will retreat to the study until dinner. “Lena, will you tell Eve to remove the flowers from Lionel’s bed? They are starting to wilt” she says before she leaves, and her voice sounds strained. Lena considers for a moment to walk after her, but she doesn’t know how she would even have a conversation with her mother, so she remains glued to her seat.

Lex and Lena sit in silence for a minute. The clock next to the fireplace strikes five and plays a short melody, then the room falls silent again. Somewhere in the house, they can hear a maid loudly running up the stairs.

“I’m sure you will enjoy life in the North,” Lex starts after a while. “We won’t live in one of the primitive huts by the creek. We will have a nice house in the town with a fireplace. Benoit and I still have claimed stakes, and just like last time we will leave most of the work to other miners and then share the gold with them. Especially now that so many new miners are going there, there won’t be much space for everyone to stake a claim. But the miners who are leaving now don’t know that, they think that gold just floats down the river in tennis-ball sized nuggets.” He laughs.

“Many miners will be desperate, and we can make a good deal with them to share our claim. You will be comfortable, Lena, and soon be married, and be friends with the other women. The air is much cleaner than in the city, and maybe you can learn a new skill to help around the house.” Lex gives her a poised smile, but Lena doesn’t return it.

“Why do you need to go back, Lex? We have all the money in the world. You could be comfortable here, in father’s study, expanding the business like you wanted to.” She says quietly.

“Because there is always more, Lena! There might be more gold in the creeks of the Yukon that we don’t know about! I want to be a part of history, Lena. This will be as big as the gold find in California fifty years ago! Gold is what moves this country, and as a businessman, I will move forward with it.” His eyes are wide as he talks, and Lena only sighs in response. Nothing she can say will make him change his mind.

She presses her lips tightly together and looks past him out of the window. She has no intention of going with Lex, but at the same time she knows she has no choice. She briefly thinks of Kara, thinks of the letter and the poem in the drawer of her vanity, and bites the inside of her cheek so hard her eyes tear up.

Despite Lex’ irrationality and obsession with finding more gold, she knows she would miss him if she didn’t go with him. Now that Lionel has passed and she has learned his secret, she simply cannot stand to think of abandoning the last blood relative she has left.

She swallows and wills away the tears in her eyes, then gets up without another word. Lex looks at her in expectation, and it makes Lena huff. Does he expect her to thank him with a hug for disregarding everything she says, thinks and feels?

“I’m feeling quite tired, so I will be in my chamber. If you see Eve, please send her my way,” she whispers and leaves before her brother can hold her back.

Once in her chamber, she stands in front of the vanity. She looks at herself in the looking glass, at her tired eyes and chapped lips. The collar of her blouse is scraping uncomfortably at her neck, and she opens two buttons, letting out a deep sigh.

She opens the drawer and fishes out Kara’s letter. Sam had delivered it a few days ago. Eve had accepted it at the front door without notifying Lena, but at least her maid had had the decency not to open and read the letter before giving it to her.

She sits down on her bed and reads the letter again. She could recite it by heart by now, and yet it still evokes the same emotions in her as the first time she had read it. Lena has always hated poetry, but there is something about reading a verse Kara wrote in her unique, messy handwriting that makes her reconsider the sentiment. The verse is simple, and Lena doesn’t know the poet or the collection Kara referenced, but it brings her back to the hours they had spent together sitting under the oak tree, telling each other their secrets.

Lena knows she needs to send a response. She misses Kara, she’s not allowed to leave the house, and their ship leaves in a week. The thought of never seeing Kara again makes her heart feel like lead in her chest. If this is the last letter she will ever send to Kara, she wants to write something profound, but it’s as if her head is devoid of any and all words. All there is, is the heavy, heavy realization that her life as she knows it is over, and it will never be the same.

She wishes she had an escape plan, that there was a way for her to run away and never come back. But Eve knows where she went the last time, and she wouldn’t know where else to go. Maybe she could write a letter to Kara once they reached Skagway, but the thought alone of Kara reading it weeks later with Lena far away makes her choked and clutch the letter so tightly its delicate edges crumble under her fingertips.

There is a knock on the door that makes her startle. “Miss Luthor? You called for me?” It’s Eve.

“Come in” Lena croaks out and quickly gets up.

“Lillian asked you remove the flowers from Lionel’s bed.” Lena says once Eve is in her chamber. Eve nods.

“Oh, and I would like for you to deliver a letter to Kara within the next days” she adds, her voice a little firmer.

Eve hesitates. “Miss Luthor, I’m not sure it would be wise of me to go to the shoemaker again. Your mother has not sent me on as many errands as usual, and I’m not sure when there would be an opportunity. I could go to your friend Samantha’s house-“

“Eve.” Lena crosses her arms. “I will write my response to Kara, and you will deliver it to her personally. I do not care if it is in two or three days, but it must be within the week. Has Lex not told you yet?”

“Has he told me what?” Eve asks, eyes wide.

Lena smirks. She likes Eve, the girl has been nothing but helpful and sympathetic towards her. However, she cannot help a certain urgency rising inside of her, a panicked consciousness that in one week, everything will have changed.

“We will leave for the Klondike in a week. Mother will stay here. Benoit talked about possibly taking one of our maids with us. Wouldn’t you like to accompany Lex, Eve? I might put in a good word for you.” She doesn’t mention the fact that no one has asked her of her opinion so far. She only needs Eve to deliver the damned letter in a timely and secretive manner.

Eve gulps. “I understand. It would be my pleasure to accompany your family up North. I will find a way to deliver the letter, Miss. Hand it to me whenever you’ve finished it.”

“Very well, thank you, Eve” Lena says and dismisses her maid with the wave of her hand.

As soon as the door is closed, she sinks down onto her bed again. Tears burn behind her eyes, but she swallows hard, keeping them from spilling over. She walks towards her vanity and takes out her writing papers. They’re under the magazines and letters from Jack, and she notices how cluttered the drawer has become. She wonders if she will take them with her up North, or if she can risk leaving them here and have Lillian snoop around the chamber in her absence.

She sighs, then spreads out the paper in front of her. She writes until she is called for dinner, even if the words that actually land on the paper are sparse. Kara has always been more eloquent with words, she thinks when she reads her clumsy attempt at laying bare her feelings.

Eve manages to deliver the letter the following Tuesday. Lena burns to know if Kara herself accepted it, but she doesn’t dare ask lest she comes across as desperate. It is decided that Eve will come with her, Lex and Benoit, and the week is spent packing suitcases with clothing, books and writing materials, and boxes with canned foods. Lena does not allow herself to think about what she’s doing, where she’s going, and how she’s feeling. She once talked to Kara about things in life that were out of their control, and so she accepts her fate and bites her tongue.

And then it’s the morning of their departure, and she has not heard back from Kara. Kara’s letter weighs heavy in her pocket as the carriage takes them from their house towards the harbor before the sun has even risen.

10. Chapter 10

Kara does her best not to make a sound as she gets out of bed and walks across the room. She knows which floorboards tend to creak, can navigate her way through the dark room perfectly. She stops for a moment. Alex is sleeping peacefully at the other side. The scene is so familiar it makes her throat feel tight.

The sun has not yet come up, but she can hear the horses on the street, and men shouting instructions to one another. While nothing unusual, this morning it hits her how used she has become to Harbor Road being busy most hours of the day, to Seattle growing each day, to the people passing through.

She quietly lets out the breath she had been holding, then continues to move around in the dimness of the room. She takes off her sleeping gown and folds it neatly onto the chair, puts on her work clothes and reaches for the small canvas bag that’s lying on the floor. She puts in a change of clothes, an extra pair of wool socks that Winn made her once, a photograph of her parents. She opens her drawer slowly to pack more of her belongings. Her poem collection, a photograph of herself and Alex, Eliza, and Jeremiah. The book Lena had given her, and her letter from a few days ago. The more she packs into it, the heavier and bulkier the bag becomes, the more she realizes how much ties her to this place, to this city.

Kara suppresses a sigh. She doesn’t have a plan, has not thought about what could go right or wrong once she leaves the house. All she knows is that she cannot stay, no matter how much she’s leaving behind. She looks over to Alex, whose arm is draped over her pillow in an uncomfortable-looking position. Kara is sure that Alex will complain about the stiffness in the morning, but she won’t be here to hear it.

She had received Lena’s letter a few days ago. It had been short. Nevertheless, she had felt the urgency of Lena’s words. Lena had told her the departure time of their steamer. She had let her know how much she loved the poem. How much she would miss Kara. How she hoped they would meet again.

The same day, Kara overheard two clients at their shop talking about the ship Lena had said the Luthors had purchased tickets for. It would be the last one before the winter, the prospective miners needed to reach the Yukon river before it froze over.

Kara hadn’t slept since that day. She had been lying awake at night, contemplating what would be best. Let Lena go with her family against her will and never see her again? Or leave behind Alex, Eliza, the shop, and the only place she had ever called home?

Kara clutches the canvas bag as she moves down the stairs. She walks into the shop. It’s even darker than the rest of the house, and she has to wait a moment for her eyes to adjust. As she stands there, she can hear footsteps on the stairs. She freezes. Had Alex woken up and noticed she had left? But the footsteps are small, quiet. Charlie. She hears him open the backdoor and walk outside. Assuming he is using the outhouse, she breathes out and walks across the shop towards the shelf.

Guilt rises up her throat as she removes the tag from one of the newly finished pairs of rubber boots. She puts them on. They are a little too big, but they will have to do. She can buy new ones when she arrives … wherever that will be. Next, she walks behind the counter and feels around for the drawer. It creaks as it opens, and Kara holds her breath, but the house remains quiet. She takes out about twenty dollars, then closes it again.

She knows that Alex will be angry and disappointed when she finds out. But Kara is determined to pay her sister back, to send a letter explaining herself once she arrives. Wherever that is, whenever that is. She can feel tears swelling behind her eyes.

“Forgive me, Alex” she murmurs.

She stays still for another minute or so, listening to the quietness of the house. Then she walks out of the backdoor. Finding it odd that Charlie has not yet come back in, she peeks around the corner. But the outhouse is empty, and Charlie isn’t there. Her heartrate picks up. Could Charlie have left at this hour? Where would he have gone?

She snorts as she realizes that she could be asking herself the same question and decides not to worry about the boy. She quietly closes the backdoor, then takes one last look at the house. She looks at the boulder she has sat on so many times, the place where she and Alex had had their conversation the other night. Biting the inside of her cheek, she averts her eyes and turns around the corner onto the street. The sun has started to rise. She looks down the street towards the harbor, where there seems to be a large crowd gathered.

Kara is about to start walking in its direction when she sees the figure of a boy rushing down the sidewalk and turning the corner a hundred or so yards away. Charlie. Against her better judgement, she hurries after him, walking as quickly as her boots allow her to. She clutches her canvas bag to her chest, already regretting packing so much into it.

When she turns the corner, she can see Charlie walking on the other side of the street. She slows down. She’s unsure if Charlie heard her moving around in the house, if he knows where she is going, if he had seen her following him. He’s not walking into the direction of the harbor, but towards the Western part of the city. He turns another street corner. By the time Kara gets there, however, he is nowhere to be seen. Kara bites her lip. She walks along the street, turns around the next corner, walks back, further down the street, turns around a different corner. Charlie, however, has disappeared.

Kara stands on the sidewalk, tears burning in her eyes. She’s just packed her belongings into a tiny bag, stolen her sister’s work and money, and followed their apprentice around when she is about to go to the harbor and leave the city. All of a sudden, she feels overwhelmed. She’s cold and hungry. She wants to turn back, to return the boots and the money and crawl back into bed in the hopes that Alex and Eliza haven’t woken up yet. But Lena’s letter weighs heavy in her bag, and she cannot stand to never see her again.

So she keeps moving, walks towards the harbor and tries to forget about Charlie, the shop, about everything she’s leaving behind. When the docks come into view, she takes in a sharp breath. The steam ship is massive, the people looking tiny next to it. A considerable crowd has gathered at the harbor, and horses and carriages are moving here and to. When she gets there, she is reminded of the morning in July when the first ship of miners arrived. The energy of the crowd is similar now, except that now miners are leaving, expecting to return with sacks of gold of their own.

There are men selling last-minute goods to the people boarding the ship. Newspapers, canned foods, tobacco, wool socks. A group of native men is standing by the dock, heavily discussing something in their own language. A woman is carrying a small child and crying as she gazes towards the steamer.

Kara walks among them, trying to catch a glimpse of the Luthors, but they are nowhere to be seen. She wonders if they have already boarded the ship, if they’re sitting comfortably in their private cabin. She walks up towards a tall man guarding the gangplank, checking the tickets of the people boarding.

“Excuse me, Sir?”

The man raises an eyebrow. “What’s the matter? You boarding?”

“Do you know where I may purchase a ticket?” Kara asks timidly.

The man lets out a loud laugh. “Oh, you’re too late, young lady. They sold out two days ago. There’s many people trying to head North before the winter.” He looks her up and down.

Kara’s shoulders sag. “You wouldn’t know where else I could ask?”

“You could try your luck asking around, maybe someone is willing to sell their ticket for double or three times the price,” the man says and laughs again, then motions for Kara to get out of the way as a young man approaches them, ticket in hand.

Kara turns around without another word. She looks towards the clock by the boardwalk. She has half an hour until the ship leaves. The sun is now up, and the crowd is getting bigger by the minute. She walks towards a man selling cigarettes.

“Excuse me, do you know where I can purchase a ticket for the steamer?”

The man laughs. “No, I’m afraid not. Haven’t seen anyone walking around selling tickets this morning, either.”

Kara sighs. “Thank you.”

She walks towards the next man, and the next, but their responses are the same. No one seems to be selling tickets. Kara curses under her breath. If only she had made a plan sooner, she would be on the steamer by now, looking out for Lena.

She returns to the gangplank. The man gives her an apprehensive look before she even opens her mouth. “Sir, is there any way for me to board the steamer? I am skilled in manual labor, I will work for a place to sleep and a simple meal.” She tries not to sound desperate, but she can feel the panic rising in her chest. She is mere minutes away from never seeing Lena again.

The man crosses his arms in front of his chest. “Sorry, sweetheart, but I’m not one to decide that, and I believe the kitchen maids are already hired. There’s no room on the ship for you. Sorry. You either have a ticket, or you’re not going on board.”

Kara bites her lip to keep herself from crying. The man ignores her and steps forward to shout towards the waiting people. “Five minutes! Five minutes left to board! The ship leaves at 8 o’clock! Be quick if you wish to board!”

Kara stands still, watches as another young man boards, and turns towards the man one last time before he himself goes onto the ship. “Please …” she pleas, but to no avail. The man ignores her. Then, the gangplank gets raised, and just like that Lena is a world away.

She looks up. People are standing on deck, waving towards the crowd on land. She tries to make out Lena or Lex among them, but the steamer is big, and the passengers seem far away. After another minute or so, the engine starts to roar, drowning out the conversations of the people around her. Smoke begins to rise through the funnels, and Kara watches as the ship begins to maneuver out of the dock.

Men and women, on deck and on land alike, begin to cheer and wave. Men raise their hats, women wave their handkerchiefs. Kara can only stand there, taking in everything and nothing all at once. The ship becomes blurry, and she realizes she is crying. She wipes her face with her sleeve.

She had come so close to leaving it all behind. Now, Lena is being taken further away from her with each passing minute. Kara starts sobbing, letting the tears fall freely this time. What does she care who sees her? The one most dear to her heart is being taken away from her. If only she could have seen Lena one last time, she would have told her how much she really means to her.

Instead, Kara now has to turn around and go home, and face the life she almost left behind. She has to face the fact that she stole from her sister, and never said goodbye. The ship is almost at the horizon. Kara takes a last, tearful look at it, then turns around and runs home.

Her bag feels heavier than before, and the too-large rubber boots make her almost trip and fall more than once. She collides with a man who shouts at her, but she doesn’t stop to apologize. She runs home, runs up Harbor Road and comes to a stop in front of the shop door, out of breath and slightly nauseous.

The shop is still closed, and so Kara walks around the house and opens the backdoor. She can hear Alex and Eliza talking animatedly in the kitchen. The guilt has a death grip on her throat by now, and she can barely breathe as she steps inside the house. When she appears on the threshold of the kitchen, touching the doorpost, the conversation abruptly comes to a halt. There is a long moment of silence in which Alex and Eliza simply stare at her before Alex jumps up and wraps her arms around her tightly.

“Where did you go? You weren’t there when I woke up. Where-“ Alex mumbles as she presses Kara against her chest. Something in the tone of her voice lets Kara know that Alex has an inkling where Kara went, or rather tried to go, but she cannot bring herself to say it out loud.

When Alex lets her go, she looks her up and down. She gulps when she sees the canvas bag that has dropped to the floor, a photograph having fallen out. Kara feels tears rising up again when Alex notices the rubber boots.

“Did you-“

“I’m sorry, Alex” Kara sobs, hiding her face in her hands.

Eliza walks over to them, and she takes Kara’s hands in hers, prying them away from her face. “You wanted to follow her, didn’t you?” she asks, softly.

Kara only nods. “I’m sorry. I didn’t have a plan. All I wanted … all I wanted …”

Eliza tugs her towards herself, hugging her tightly. “It’s alright, Kara. When you weren’t here this morning, I thought you had boarded the ship.
