She and Kara sit down on the weekend to write to their loved ones in Seattle – Kara a long, long letter to Alex and Eliza, Lena the postcard she bought in Chicago to Sam and Ruby. On weekdays, Kara leaves the house in the morning with Jack to go to the office. Lena leaves sometime after them, making it her mission to browse as many city directories in different districts as she can get her hands on.

Often, she brings lunch to the office, and Kara excitedly talks about the typewriter or an advertisement she found in the papers. Lena browses newspapers, too, for any information about the gold miners in the Yukon, or maybe a personal ad Lex might have posted.

On a rainy Tuesday, she storms into Jack’s office with sandwiches from the lunch wagon, and a huge smile on her face.

“I found her!” she exclaims, catching her breath. She puts her umbrella into the corner. “I think I found Jess’ address; I will send her a letter today!”

She stops dead in her tracks when she sees Jack and Kara standing in the middle of the room, looking at her with a blank expression. A feeling of imminent dread washes over her.

“Is something wrong?” she asks at the same time as Kara says “That’s great!”

“You should read this.” Jack hands her a newspaper, finger pointing at an article.

Lena’s heart skips a beat. Lex has found me, she thinks. He will have me come back, he will ruin my life, and Jack’s and Kara’s along with it. Oh, God.

She reads the headline.

WAREHOUSE WORKER FATALLY INJURED

She squints. Thinking it’s the wrong article she scans the page for another one, until Luthor Luxury Imports catches her eye, just under that same headline.

Two years after the formation of a union of warehouse workers that includes workers at Luthor Luxury Imports, a 20-year-old worker suffered fatal injuries yesterday when handling heavy equipment. The workers have long lamented unsafe working conditions and long hours as well as low wages. Representatives of the LLI office in New York were unavailable for comment after a request. This news comes only weeks after the passing of entrepreneur Lionel Luthor. Lex, his son and successor, has allegedly left his current residence in Seattle for the Yukon, where he had been mining for gold since last year. Workers are threatening to strike should their demands not be met by next week.

Lena needs another moment to stare at the article until she can speak. “Oh, this is not good. Oh, Lillian will be furious. She was always against Lex leaving, even last year, but all he talked about was making the company even more prestigious, earn more status.”

“It’s not good. I had no idea about the union,” Jack says, nodding in sympathy.

“Oh, my family has always completely disregarded their demands. A bottle of our imported perfume sells for more than their monthly wages, and neither Lionel nor Lex have ever cared,” Lena huffs, crumbling the newspaper in her hand.

Jack scratches his mustache. “The incident definitely draws some unwanted attention to your family. We need to look even closer, if there’s any mention of you in the papers.”

Lena looks at Kara, who has been quiet. Kara walks over to her, putting a hand on her shoulder. “What are you going to do?” she whispers.

“I just …” she says. Her eyes fill with tears. “I feel so horrible. My family has always been so cruel, and now Lex let his obsession with gold get ahold of him, bringing bad headlines for the company. And Lillian is left to handle it, while she’s still in mourning.”

“Do you want to go back to Seattle?” Jack asks.

Lena shakes her head, letting out a low laugh. “No, absolutely not. I’d just be flying back into my old birdcage, and who would it help?” She sighs. “No. I need to use my family name for something good, for once. Jack, can we find out the name of the worker who died?”

Jack sags his shoulders. “Yes, that should be doable. But Lena, please. Do not go to the warehouses, or to the office. It’s dangerous for you, once they learn who you are. I will look into it. What do you want to know?”

Averting her gaze from Kara, Lena bites her lip. “I just want to know if he had a family. He was only twenty, the same age as me. I wish to pay for the funeral, make sure his family gets by. I have no influence in the company, but maybe I can help him in that way. Use my money for something good, for once.”

“I think that’s a good idea, Lena” Kara says, and Jack nods.

“Agreed. Now, why don’t you two go home? You can have the rest of the day off, Kara. The typewriter can wait until tomorrow. Adam should be in the study, or on a small errand.” He takes the newspaper back from Lena, smoothing out the edge.

“Thank you for bringing lunch, Lena. I’m sorry to interrupt your good mood like this. I’m happy you found Jess’ address, you should write to her soon!” He gives her an encouraging smile.

Lena presses her lips together. Her earlier good mood has completely evaporated, the triumph of finding Jess overshadowed by the tragic death of the worker. She wants to curse Lex, curse her father and all their gold.

Lex would always tell her she has a bleeding heart, but she’ll be damned if she ever becomes like him. She doesn’t know the worker, but the knowledge that her family hasn’t cared about the conditions in their warehouses for years makes her chest heavy with guilt.

She and Kara haul a carriage to take them home. The ride is short and silent. Adam is indeed in the study. They decide to eat lunch together, Lena and Kara their sandwiches, Adam something the maid prepared for him. While they eat, they tell him about the article. Lena has no appetite, and she chugs the bites down with large amounts of coffee. Naturally, it does nothing to help her calm down.

“Lena, it’s going to be alright. You’ll find his family, and compensate them.” Kara says as she puts a reassuring hand on her shoulder.

Adam nods. “She’s right. We can talk about it tonight when Jack comes home.” He gives her a sympathetic smile. “I can go to the warehouse to ask around, if needed. We’ll do this together, alright?”

Lena only nods. She’s only been to the warehouses once, when she was twelve or thirteen, together with her father. There’s an office close by that their representative has always used, Lionel preferring the quiet of his study over the rowdy waterfront. It’s a muddy and dirty place in her memory, and she doubts it has changed much.

“Yes. Thank you, truly.” She whispers. Kara’s hand is warm and soft on her shoulder. She leans into it a little, craving the comfort it brings her.

She keeps busy during the day. Once the rain has subsided, Kara accompanies her to the bank, where she exchanges her gold nuggets into dollars. Kara’s eyes almost bulge out of her head when she sees the bank notes.

Lena wouldn’t admit it, but there’s an unease that takes hold of her when her thumb runs across the edge of the stack. Seeing the value of the gold right in front of her makes it surreal. She’d been carrying a smaller nugget in her purse since Lex had come back, like a child that had collected a sea-shell. The thought that this, a metal her brother found in a stream faraway, has suddenly transformed into something tangible, something she can use, makes her shudder.

Once they’re back at home, she distracts herself by writing to Jess. It takes her five tries, her indecision and frustration evident in the crumbled-up writing papers on the table. Jess used to be her best friend, and her only one. Now she worries she might not remember her, or care for her. She worries that Jess is now the best friend of some other wealthy, lonely girl, braiding her hair and tightening her corset, listening to her cry about a gentleman she fancies.

Only, Lena had never cried about gentlemen. She hopes to ask Jess to work for them, in Jack’s house, and she wonders when she reads the letter over that Jess will see behind the façade that her and Jack’s marriage will be. Would Jess approve of Kara? She sighs and puts the letter into an envelope. There is only one way to find out, by sending it on its way, and hoping she will hear back.

 

***

 

It’s while they’re getting ready for sleep in the evening that Lena gets lost in her thoughts once more. They’re in their nightgowns, Lena brushing Kara’s hair, who sits on the chair in front of the vanity. Kara is pulling the last of her hair pins out of her hair and placing them in the old sourball tin box.

The first time Lena saw it on the vanity, she’d thought Kara had bought the candy during the day, before realizing what it contained. She remembered Kara’s excitement when she’d given her the sourballs, remembered her amazement of such a simple act bringing her such joy. She wanted to offer to Kara to buy her a new box to keep her hair pins in, maybe a Japanese lacquer box that would also fit jewelry. But she’d never seen Kara wear jewelry, and something held her back from asking.

Kara’s hair is soft and wavy, flowing through her hands like silk. Kara hums at the contact of Lena’s fingertips on her scalp, at the strokes of the hairbrush. Lena spends more time on it than necessary, but neither of them says anything. She looks at Kara through the looking glass, and finds she has her eyes closed.

Of all the things the gold has taken from her – her father’s health first and then his life, her brother’s attention and companionship, the city she had begun to love – it has given her more blessings than she can count. It’s given her Kara, first and foremost. And even though the waters are a little rough now, she hopes to one day lead an exciting and happy life in the city she grew up in, with a freedom she would have never enjoyed had she not stepped down from the steamer. The steamer that would have taken her towards more gold, in an ironic twist of fate.

She’s pulled out of her thoughts when Kara’s fingers brush her hand, and realizes she has stopped her motions.

“Are you alright?” Kara asks, small smile on your lips. “What’s on your mind? The incident from the article?”

Lena shakes her head. She puts the brush down, running a hand through her own hair. “Just thinking. About the gold, about everything. I just …” she sighs.

Kara looks at her then, not through the looking glass, but directly, tilting her head. Her smile is warm, as are her fingertips, and the look in her eyes. Lena takes a moment to bask in it like Kara is pure sunlight, like a cat on a windowsill in the spring.

“I just want to help people. I can’t undo the wrongdoings of my family, but … I want to do good. And I don’t know-“ her lip starts quivering, and she doesn’t know what she wants to say.

“Shh,” Kara shushes her, and then tugs on her sleeve, gently pulling her into her lap. Lena lets her, and Kara wraps her arms around her tightly.

“You are good, Lena” Kara mumbles, close to her ear. “You are good. You’ve always helped people, always shown that you care. You’ve helped me and my family, you’ve always treated those who work for you well, and you will help the family of the worker from the warehouse.”

Lena swallows thickly. She wants to say something, to protest that she’s done nothing of value for twenty years, but Kara continues. “You never judged me, no matter how much or little you knew about me. You were my friend when I was just a shoemaker, and you are here with me still now. And I just know that you will excel at college, and become someone great that people will admire and write about in books one day.”

“Kara-“ Lena’s breath hitches in her throat, and she fights tears from forming behind her closed eyelids. They’ve exchanged all sorts of tender words and touches, but Lena has never felt more secure, has never wanted to believe anything as much as she wants to believe Kara’s affirmations in this moment.

She leans more into Kara’s touch, wants to sink deeper into the warmth of her very being. If Kara is the sun, she thinks, Lena is the moon, shining only in her light.

Kara hums, tightening her hold on her, and they sit like that until Kara complains about her leg falling asleep with a chuckle. Once they lay down to sleep, Lena lets herself be held. Kara, in turn, holds onto her as if she was afraid of Lena slipping away. Her grip only loosens as she falls asleep, and Lena drifts off as well, their breaths even and in sync, Kara’s touch warm and comforting.

 

***

 

Kara cannot recall the last time she got out of bed each morning with a swing in her step, ready to face the day. Every day in New York seems to bring about a new adventure. There’s new food to eat, new streets and parks to explore, and most excitingly, more to learn on the typewriter, and the general workings of Jack’s office.

When Adam returns from his trip, Kara and him become friends almost instantly. She misses Winn and James, but she finds that Jack’s partner is just as friendly and conversational as them. Like James, he is a member of a photography club. He shows her his photography album of London, and teaches her how to use his Kodak. Kara takes it all in with enthusiasm.

On a sunny Sunday, they’re off to take a walk around town. Kara has borrowed Adam’s Kodak, and is adamant about getting everyone’s picture in their Sunday best. She takes photographs of Jack and Adam in front of the house, then has her and Lena’s photographs taken, as well. She plans on taking pictures of the streets, the house, their chambers, Jack’s office, wants to capture everything so Alex and Eliza may get to see her new life.

When the film roll finally arrives in the mail, she almost drops it in excitement. It’s a Saturday, and she and Lena are in the sitting room, reading and drinking coffee. The house feels more and more like home each day, and the sitting room provides a welcome escape from the lingering heat of the late summer.

She opens the box with some difficulty. Lena puts down the paper she had been reading to peek over her shoulder. Kara takes each photograph into the palm of her hand. Logically, she knows that photographs are nothing special anymore, at least not to the extent that they were when she was a child. Nevertheless, the lightness of the cardboard in her hand feels special to her. She was the one capturing the moment pictured, like a memory of hers frozen in time.

“They turned out really well” Lena says with a smile. “You’re savvy.” She takes one out of Kara’s hands.

Kara blushes. She picks up the next, which is of her and Lena in front of the house, taken by Jack. She takes a long moment to look at it. She’s wearing one of the dresses Lena had bought for her at the tailor, smiling wide. Lena’s hands are on the small of her back, her smile matching Kara’s. It’s a good photograph, their first one together.

“We should put this on the shelf!” Lena suggests.

Her face is close, Kara can feel her breath on her neck. She nods. The bookshelf in their chamber, that is no longer the guest room, and that is no longer empty. Kara’s books pile up there, next to the photographs she had taken with her from Seattle. Lena owns very few sentimental items, and her suggestion makes Kara’s chest swell with joy.

“That’s a great idea.” She smiles and turns her head, pressing a kiss to Lena’s lips.

Lena hums and smiles wide against her lips so that their teeth clash. It makes Kara giggle and pull away. Lena shifts and guides Kara’s face towards her again with a finger on her cheek, then kisses her again. Kara’s hands wander to Lena’s waist, pressing her hands into the soft skin underneath, pulling her closer.

Kara scoots over to make more room for Lena, but is startled when instead something crumbles loudly. She pulls apart from Lena with a frustrated groan, but Lena only giggles. Looking down, she realizes she is sitting on the paper Lena had been reading. A photograph has fallen to the floor.

Kara rushes to pick it up and smooth out the crinkles, but instead smears ink across the page due to her clammy hands. Lena laughs and takes it from her.

“I found this weekly series that I thought might interest you, before you made it all illegible!” Her tone is playful, failing to scold Kara despite her narrowed eyes. She laughs again, and Kara can’t help but join her.

She pushes a strand of hair out of her face, forgetting about the ink on her fingers. Lena gasps and grasps her wrist, turning her palm upwards. With the other, she rubs on the black stripe of ink on Kara’s forehead.

“God, you’re so clumsy!” She shakes her head in faux-annoyance. “I can’t believe Jack lets you operate his typewriter.”

Kara blushes again and bites her lips. She has no answer, so she watches as Lena dips her handkerchief into a glass of water and then wipes Kara’s fingers clean. Her motions are gentle, her hand around her wrist warm.

To distract herself, Kara asks: “So, what is the story about?”

“Martians invading New York” Lena deadpans, and Kara stares at her.

“Is that true?”

Lena snorts. “The Martians? I hope not.” She lets go of Kara’s hand and neatly folds her handkerchief back into her pocket. “I didn’t read all of it, and it’s not the first part, unfortunately. But it’s from the author who wrote the time travel story, so I thought you might like it.”

Kara hums. She remembers Lena giving her the book about the time machine, and them discussing the possibilities of such a device. It was only a year ago, but it feels like in a different lifetime already. Her childhood home had been full of novels and other books. Living in this house now, with a bright study and shelves with more books that she can ever hope to read or understand, brings her a sort of comfort she hadn’t been aware she had been missing.

They can hear Jack and Adam entering the house from the back porch, their boots heavy on the wooden floor boards. They’d hired a gardener to create flower beds, since the previous owners had left the small backyard mostly untouched. Kara can already imagine the evenings they will spend on the porch by candlelight, talking and playing card games.

The two men enter the sitting room, greeting Lena and Kara. Jack smiles when he sees the mess of coffee cups and scattered photographs. “How did they turn out?” he asks, nodding towards the table.

“Most excellent!” Lena replies. She adds, “Say, Jack, have you got last week’s edition of the Evening Journal? There’s a chapter of a story I am looking for.”

Adam perks up. “They’re publishing Fighters from Mars, are they not? Oh, you should read the novel instead, I have it somewhere!”

Kara blinks in confusion, but Jack simply laughs. “Oh, he has an opinion or two about that series! It was published in London a few years back, I believe. The setting is of course London, and the title is War of the Worlds. They’ve changed it for publication here in the States, but he much prefers the original.” He pats his partner’s shoulder in amusement.

Adam’s eyes narrow. “It’s true. I will find the novel for you, Kara, and I’d like to hear your opinion on it. I do find it hard to believe that alien species exist and that they will care about Earth at all. But then again, outer space is wide, so how can we ever be sure? There’s so much we don’t know.”

He sighs, running a hand through his hair. “Say, have you read The Time Machine?”

Jack throws his head back, groaning in response, but Lena and Kara laugh.

 

***

 

A week or so later, Kara yelps as soon as she and Lena enter the door. Lena startles next to her, but Kara dashes towards the highboy. There, in Alex’ unmistakable, messy handwriting, is a letter addressed to Kara Zoller. She picks it up as if it was an expensive piece of china, and her eyes go wide when she sees the letter underneath. It’s addressed to Lena Luthor in bold, angry letters.

She holds it out towards Lena, who approaches her. Her brows furrow when she turns it around. “From my mother,” she says in a toneless voice.

“How does she know-“ Lena takes in a sharp breath. “Of course. Jack sent me a letter when he just moved here once. She must have copied his address.”

She laughs, but it’s dry and devoid of humor. “Of course, she knows where I went. I’ve never been able to hide from her. I’ll never escape the Luthors.”

Kara’s chest tightens, her own excitement forgotten. Whatever is written in the letter, it can’t be good.

“From Alex?” Lena asks, a weak smile on her lips as she nods at the letter in Kara’s hands.

Kara only nods. “We have some time until dinner. Shall we go upstairs and read the letters together?”

“Alright.”

They plop down onto the ottoman after Kara carelessly throws the blouses and corset lying on it onto the bed. Lena is quiet, and Kara can practically hear her worries riding a Ferris Wheel inside of her head. She gently takes the letter out of her hand and opens it with her thumb, then gives it back to her.

“You first. Whatever is in there, you can share it with me, if you’d like to.”

Lena sits still. She makes no move to read the letter, but her hands are trembling. After another minute or so, she unfolds it. Her expression is void of any emotion as she reads. When she finishes, she folds it again and huffs.

“Well, some news, I suppose. Other than that, nothing she’s never said to me before.” She gives the letter to Kara without looking at her.

Kara swallows thickly. She had offered to read it, but now she’s not sure she wants to hear what Lillian has to say about Lena.

Lena,

With dismay I have learned that you somehow left the steamer to Skagway and did not accompany your brother and fiancé to the Yukon. I find it most regrettable that you would undermine your family’s wishes in such a lowly way. Since you did not return to the house, I assumed this is where this letter may find you.

Firstly, Lex’s letter came with the announcement of Benoit’s annulment of your engagement. He remains faithful to our company and family, however, which I took note of with great relief. He seems to have expressed interest in marrying Miss Teschmacher, which I find surprising, however not disagreeable.

I have taken the liberty to remove your name from your father’s will, as I’m sure you will be fine without the assets. Further, I received a telegram from our representative in New York about an anonymous donator for the deceased warehouse worker’s funeral. Since you must have surely heard of the accident, I cannot imagine someone else giving the matter a second thought. Sentimentality has always been your weakness, and I must ask you to stay out of LLI’s business. I am handling the matter just fine.

While I lament the regrettable way in which you have chosen to depart this family, I wish you well.

Lillian

 

Kara takes a deep breath. She has never read a letter that has made her blood boil more than this. She gets up, making a fist. Lena, however, remains quiet. She looks up at Kara with a sad look.

“Kara, it’s alright. They won’t come after us, and that’s all I need to know. I’ll marry Jack, I don’t need their assets. It’s alright.”

“No, Lena” Kara implores, chin quivering. “I can’t-“ She doesn’t know what to say.

They’re quiet for a moment, staring at each other.

“The Luthor name doesn’t deserve you, Lena” Kara whispers. She crumbles the letter between her fingers, then walks across the room to throw it into the empty fire place.

She sits back down next to Lena, wrapping her arms around her. Lena’s breath hitches, the only sign she’s been affected by her mother’s words in any way since she opened the letter. She places a hand over Kara’s.

“I’m happy you’re here with me, Kara.” Lena’s voice is barely above a whisper.

Kara swallows thickly. “I’m happy to be here with you, too.”

They sit in silence until they’re called for dinner. Kara spends it shifting in her seat, barely listening to the conversations around her. Lillian’s letter has left her churning, but at the same time she wants to read Alex’ letter as soon as possible.

When they’re finally back upstairs, the sun has begun to set and she turns on the lamp. Lena sits down in front of the vanity, undoing her hair and watching Kara through the looking glass. Kara sits down on the ottoman, glancing at Lena. She always looks beautiful with her hair down, she thinks. Lena gives her an encouraging smile.

Alex’ letter is three pages long, and Kara’s head starts swimming once she refamiliarizes herself with her sister’s handwriting.

Dear Kara,

I was so happy to receive your letter. New York sounds exciting, and I can’t wait to visit you one day. We already miss you here. There have been many developments since you’ve been gone, and I wish you were here to witness them.

Do you remember father’s friend, Jones? He and father used to play baseball together, and I was friends with his daughter when we were very little. He came by our shop a week ago. It was the most surreal thing. He asked for Jeremiah, and I had to explain everything to him. He looked quite sorrowful, hearing of father’s passing. He stayed for dinner, however, and talked at length to Eliza.

Kara closes the letter in her lap. She had barely known Jones, but reading about him wishing to visit Jeremiah makes her tear up. She wipes at her eyes with her sleeves. Lena walks over to her and sits down next to her, putting a reassuring hand on her arm.

After a while, Kara takes a deep breath and continues to read. She allows Lena to read along over her shoulder.

Jones came to Seattle to board a steamer for Alaska, but as you know the last one had already left to make it to the Yukon before the winter. He was looking for work, so we hired him until he can go North in the spring. He’s friendly with Charlie and quite skilled.

Now that I have less work at the shop, I feel that I can start something new. I have some money saved, and I long for a new beginning. The shop is not the same without you getting on my nerves … You know I used to dream of doing the same work as mother, when father was still here. I will start my training at the hospital in October. I cannot wait.

I can hardly believe that all of these events have been set off by a steamer arriving here with gold. It will never be the same, but I couldn’t be more excited about it. I’m excited for you, Kara, and the life you started in New York. And I’m so happy that I can start the job I always dreamed to do.

And we have Charlie. He sleeps in your bed now, I hope that is alright. He’s making progress with his ABCs, and he is very good with numbers. I will start him on the register soon. He talks about you often, he misses you, too. He plays dominoes with Winn now. We all miss you terribly.

I should also mention that I have been spending a lot of time with Sam. She and Ruby miss Lena most terribly, as well. We talk about the two of you often, and Ruby likes to remind us it was her and Charlie who saved you both from a life in misery.

I await your next letter in excitement. I hope you are well and comfortable, and cannot wait until I see you again! We all miss you.

Alex

 

She folds the letter in her lap. The chamber becomes blurry from her tears, but she makes no move to wipe them. Lena gently cups her chin, wiping away her tears with her thumb. She pulls Kara into a hug.

“They miss you a lot.” Lena whispers. Kara only nods.

“I’m sorry, Kara” she adds and gulps. Kara is unsure what Lena could be sorry for, but then she continues. “I’m sorry for taking you away from them. I … I left my family behind knowing I’d be happier without them, but you … Alex must despise me for taking you away.”

Kara puts a hand on Lena’s shoulder, making her turn to look at her. “That is not true. I went with you because I wanted to, because the life I lived there was never the one meant for me. Eliza said so, too. Alex knows it, too. And she’s finally breaking free from her old shackles, as well.”

She sighs. Lena looks at her with wide eyes. “One thing I’ve learned now is that change is good when you’re the one pushing for it. Both of us have had things happen in our lives that we couldn’t control, and we’ve made do with it. But now we can finally make our own choices, and I don’t want you to think that you took my choice away from me. I chose you, Lena.”

Lena takes in a sharp breath. Her eyes are darting across Kara’s face. Kara smiles at her. All she can do is hope that Lena believes her. Finally, Lena nods and closes her eyes. She leans her forehead against Kara’s shoulder.

“I’m happy for Alex. Truly. And I’m happy you’re here with me.” Kara pulls her closer, kissing her hair.

They’re silent for a long moment. Lena’s breathing has evened, and she’s relaxing against Kara. Kara’s head is swimming with the news from Alex’ letter. She misses her family. Nevertheless, she knows she made the right choice going with Lena, and she knows Alex thinks so, too.

“I could visit them in the spring” she says after a while. “We already did the train journey, it’s not impossible. It’s not like when our parents were young. I can stay for a few weeks, then come back.”

Lena pulls away, smiling. “That’s a good idea. I don’t want you to feel like you can never see them again. You’re right, the journey is long and exhausting, but it’s far more enjoyable than it used to be.”

She bites her lip, expression turning serious. “As long as you come back to me-“ she laughs it off with a chuckle, but Kara doesn’t miss the undertone of fear hiding behind it.

“You know I will.” Her hands find Lena’s and wrap around them.

Lena shudders, inching closer to Kara’s face.

“Promise?”

“Promise.”

 

***

 

Slowly, they begin to establish new habits and routines. Kara becomes more comfortable in the city and starts to get used to the many amenities the house has to offer. Lena and Jack make plans to marry in December before Christmas. The tension Lena had been holding inside herself begins to fade, Lillian’s letter having given her a sort of strange reassurance that this new life of theirs won’t suddenly be taken away from them again.

Kara and Adam discuss books over dinner, and Jack and Lena discuss colleges. The maid remains cold and distant, never speaking or doing more than what is asked of her, even when Jack raises her wage now that she has to wash more plates and pick up more laundry.

Kara has nearly forgotten about Jess when one night, their maid calls for a visitor at the door. Lena is the first to get up, and she squeals so loudly that Kara comes rushing after her. She sees Lena hugging another woman, both of them talking animatedly when they separate.

“Kara, come meet Jess!” Lena waves as she helps the woman inside.

It’s only then that Kara sees the woman’s belly, heavy with child. They lead her to the sitting room. Jess hasn’t worked in a while, she says, relying on her husband’s meager income while waiting for their first child. Lena’s eyes keep wandering back and forth between Jess’ face and belly in wonder, and Kara catches a glimpse of the close friendship they used to share.

It’s obvious that replacing their maid with Jess has to wait, but Lena doesn’t seem to mind. They promise to visit Jess soon, and the woman clutches Lena’s hands in hers when they say goodbye an hour later.

The summer winds down, and Kara observes Rosh Hashanah quietly. Sitting in the upstairs chamber, she includes her photographs in her letter to Alex and looks out of the window. She remembers the year she lost her parents, celebrating the holiday with the Danvers for the first time. If only her younger self could see her now, she thinks, a small smile on her lips.

Her birthday, on the other hand, is spent with the four of them, lively and not at all lonely. Jack refuses to tell a pleading Kara where they are headed as they climb into the carriage waiting in front of their door. She’s wearing her new trousers, something she’s never done outside of the house, and Lena’s hands linger on her thighs throughout the short ride.

The restaurant they arrive in front of makes Kara’s jaw drop. The three-story building has a terrace on which a small group of patrons is eating and laughing. The lanterns decorating the front look exotic, as do the red tiles and golden letters.

“The restaurant has only opened this year, and it has the finest Chinese food in all of New York” Jack grins as they step out of the carriage and he tips the coachman.

Kara has only been to this part of town once, but she has never tried the food. As if on cue, her stomach growls. Next to her, Lena laughs.

They’re led to a small private dining room with a round table. Kara gingerly touches the thick, starched table-cloth as they sit down. Jack orders liquors and dishes Kara has never heard of, but when they arrive, she is determined to try them all.

Adam grins when he hands her a pair of chopsticks. “You’ll figure them out.”

Kara stares at him, weighing the unfamiliar utensils in her hand, unsure what to do. She looks over at Lena, who seems to be struggling just as much.

“Lena, you’ve never had Chinese food before?” Jack asks from across the table, scandalized.

Lena shakes her head, laughing. “The Luthors aren’t exactly known for being receptive to new customs and cultures, Jack.”

She raises an eyebrow. “How come you two are so well-versed in it?”

Adam grins. “Well, I do consider myself somewhat of a culinary expert-“

“- which is a bold statement for a gentleman from London” Jack intercepts, and the table erupts into laughter. A good-natured argument follows, and Kara is quietly thankful for the privacy that the room provides them with.

She somewhat manages to pick up a dumpling. Taking a tentative bite, she closes her eyes as she chews. It’s good, unlike any food she has ever tasted before.

“This is the most excellent food I’ve ever had” she announces, already picking up the next one. Jack looks triumphant, and Lena purses her lips.

“Don’t tell Eliza” Kara adds quickly, with her mouth full.

Lena giggles at that, and Kara knows that this is her best birthday in many, many years. When they finish their meal, Jack announces they are not quite headed home yet. Kara can’t imagine what else he possibly might have planned, but Lena takes her arm, and they walk from the restaurant through the lively streets.

During the twenty minutes or so that they walk, Jack becomes increasingly quiet, even seeming nervous. Lena walks up to him, whispering something Kara can’t quite make out. Her confusion only increases when they turn a corner, then another, and then stop in front of an unimposing, wooden door at the side of a closed shop entrance.

Jack makes sure nobody is on the street when they enter. Kara’s palms become sweaty, and her nervousness only increases as they walk down a dark staircase until they come to a halt in front a second door. Jack knocks, and a small window opens, a man barely visible behind it. He mumbles something, to which Jack replies, and then the door is being unlocked and they are ushered in.

Jack turns to Kara with a serious expression. “Adam and I frequent this place. You and Lena are more than welcome here; however, you are not to mention it in a letter or a telegram, or loudly in conversation where others might hear. Agreed?”

Kara nods silently, and Jack pats her shoulder. When her eyes adjust to the dim light, she begins to understand the reason behind Jack’s request. A band on a small stage is playing a fast-paced song, and men and women are dancing in front of it.

The dancers, she realizes, consist of men with powdered faces and red lips, rivaling those of the finest ladies in town. Some wear wigs and dresses, Kara finds after watching the small crowd for a moment. They look carefree and energetic, and it makes Kara heart pound harder in her chest along with the rhythm of the music.

Jack motions for the four of them to walk towards the bar. Sitting down on the low stool, Kara cannot seem to stop her gaze from wandering over the patrons. In a corner, two people are having a conversation, leaning into each other and talking over the music close to their ears. It takes Kara a moment to realize that one of them is not a man, but in fact a woman in blazer and trousers much like the ones she is wearing. Her hand is resting on the waist of the woman, who laughs at something that was said.

Kara turns to Lena, who sips at her drink and winks at her. She blushes and picks up her own drink she hadn’t noticed had been put onto the counter. Jack and Adam seem lost in their own conversation next to them. Lena leans towards her.

“How do you enjoy this place so far?”

Kara bites her lip. “I had no idea such a bar existed! Everybody looks so care-free, I never-“ she is unsure how to finish her statement.

She’s never left the house in trousers before, and she hasn’t even had the time to truly think about how good, how freeing it felt, until she came here and saw another woman in trousers as if it was the most natural thing in the world. It had never even crossed her mind that there were men who found the very trousers that she longed to wear limiting, the cosmetics she avoided appealing to themselves.

She meets Lena’s eyes. Lena places her hands on hers, and her eyes are full of adoration and love. Kara wonders if she’s allowed to kiss Lena in this strange place, or if she needs to wait for the privacy of their chamber regardless.

She looks around the bar. The bartender is pouring drinks for other patrons on the other end of the counter. There’s a selection of bottles and glasses on a shelf by the wall, as well as a register. Several framed photographs hang next to it, and Kara observes them with interest. Lena follows her gaze. Her jaw drops, and she points at one of the photographs in the left corner excitedly.

“Look, Kara! It’s the dancer I saw in Paris!”

Sure enough, Kara recognizes the woman in her stage outfit, the butterfly wings taking up most of the space in the frame. A date is scribbled in the corner, and Kara guesses that it’s from a performance in the city. She wonders if the dancer and her sweetheart ever frequented this bar.

Before she can voice her thoughts, however, the current song ends, and the band starts playing a different one, slower this time. Lena reaches out her hand with a grin.

“Would you like a dance, Miss Danvers?” Her tone is playful, a mischievous glint in her eyes.

Kara puts her glass down. The way Lena looks at her makes her heart jump in her throat. She glances towards the dancefloor. The two women she had observed before have started to dance.  Their arms are tangled, a hand is low on the other’s hips.

She meets Lena’s gaze and nods, blushing as she takes her hand. She giggles.

“With you always, Miss Luthor.”
